| 68 | DB query DSL v1 — `Query.from`/`.filter`/`.orderBy`/`.limit`/`.offset`/`.sql`/`.list`/`.first`/`.count`; table/column/operator/direction literals verified against schema at compile time (E0508-E0512) | `crates/dbschema`, `crates/stdlib`, `crates/cli`, `crates/codegen` |
| 69 | Joins — `Query.join`/`.leftJoin("Table", "Base.col", "Table.col")`. ON columns must be qualified (E0518); qualifier must name the base table or an already-joined table (E0517); a bare `.filter`/`.orderBy` column ambiguous across joined tables is rejected (E0515). Self-joins (same table twice with an alias) not supported yet. | `crates/dbschema`, `crates/stdlib` |
| 70 | Aggregations — `Query.groupBy`/`.aggregate("count"\|"sum"\|"avg"\|"min"\|"max", col, alias)`/`.having`, read via `.groupedList` (no `DbRow` bound — result shape is synthetic); plus ungrouped scalar `Query.sum`/`.avg`/`.min`/`.max`. `.list`/`.first`/`.count`/scalar aggregates are rejected at compile time on an already-grouped/aggregated query (E0516), since they assume `SELECT *`/single-scalar shape. | `crates/dbschema`, `crates/stdlib` |
| 71 | Mutation builder — `Mutation.insertInto`/`.updateTable`/`.deleteFrom`/`.insertMany(table, columns)`/`.set`/`.filter`/`.onConflict`/`.addRow`/`.run`. Table/column/operator/column-list literals verified against schema at compile time (E0519, reusing E0509-E0511 from `Query`); each method restricted to the mutation kinds it's valid for — `.set` on insert/update, `.filter` on update/delete, `.onConflict` on insert (upsert), `.addRow` on insertMany — misuse rejected at compile time (E0520) rather than failing at the database; `.addRow`'s arity checked against `.insertMany`'s declared columns when both are literal (E0521). `insertMany` batches all rows into one round trip. | `crates/dbschema`, `crates/stdlib`, `crates/cli`, `crates/codegen` |
| 72 | Live schema-sync — opt-in via `[database] schema-sync = true` in `certo.toml` (reads `DATABASE_URL` like `certo db pull`, requires `psql` on PATH). Cross-checks every `type X = {...}` with `impl DbRow for X {}` against a live `information_schema.columns` snapshot: missing table (E0522), missing column (E0523), type mismatch (E0524), nullability mismatch (E0525). Table/column names bridged via the same `snake_case` ↔ `PascalCase`/`camelCase` convention `certo db pull` already uses. Off by default — a normal `certo build`/`check` never touches the network; only `DbRow`-annotated types are checked, not every declared record. | `crates/dbschema`, `crates/cli` |
| 69b | Self-joins — `Query.fromAs`/`.joinAs`/`.leftJoinAs(table, alias, ...)` give a table an explicit alias, so the same table can appear twice (e.g. employees joined to their own managers). Alias must be a valid identifier (E0514) and not already in use in this query (E0526) — which is also what makes a plain, non-aliased `.join` of the same table twice a clear compile error instead of silently broken SQL. `.from`/`.join`/`.leftJoin` are unchanged for the common case (alias defaults to the table name). | `crates/dbschema`, `crates/stdlib`, `crates/cli`, `crates/codegen` |
| 97 | Parser bug fixed — parenthesized arrow-lambda expressions (`(x) => expr`, `(a, b) => expr`, `(a: Int) => expr`, `() => expr`) didn't parse **at all**, not just the multi-param case as first logged — the spec's own single-param example (`List.map(xs, (x) => x * 2)`) was broken too. Root cause: `parse_atom` had zero lookahead for this form; only the trailing-block `{ x => }` and explicit `fn(x: T): R = ...` lambda syntaxes existed. Fixed with a balanced-paren scan (`peek_is_arrow_lambda`) that detects `=>` immediately after a matching `)` — unambiguous, since a parenthesized value/tuple is never legally followed by `=>` anywhere else in the grammar. 7 new parser tests (single/multi/typed/zero-param/curried lambdas, plus a regression guard that ordinary `(expr)`/tuples still parse). | `crates/parser` |
| 98 | Typeck + codegen bug fixed — `List.empty()`/`Map.empty()` failed to type-check (misreported as an unapplied `() => U` function). Root cause: both were registered in `seed.rs` as bare polymorphic *values* (`Forall<T>. List<T>`) instead of zero-arg *functions* (`Forall<T>. () => List<T>`) — every other zero-arg stdlib function was correctly wrapped, these two were the only exceptions. Fixing the registration then exposed a second, latent codegen bug: `certo_list_empty` was a `#define certo_list_empty certo_list_new_empty()` macro assuming bare-reference use, which double-expanded once codegen correctly started emitting a real call `certo_list_empty()`. Replaced the macro with real `certo_list_empty`/`certo_map_empty` C functions. 2 new stdlib regression tests asserting the registered type is actually `Fn{params: [], ..}`, not a bare value. | `crates/stdlib`, `crates/codegen` |
| 99 | Bug fixed — multi-param function *type* annotations `(Int, Int) => Int` parsed the parenthesized group as a single tuple-typed parameter (`((Int, Int)) => Int`), not two params | First found while testing #97/#98 (workaround at the time: omit the annotation and let inference figure out param types); fixed in a later session. Root cause: `parse_type.rs`'s `A => B` sugar always wrapped the LHS as one param regardless of whether it was itself a parenthesized list — `(Int, Int)` had already been parsed as a `TypeExpr::Tuple` by `parse_tuple_type` before the `=>` sugar ever saw it, so it got wrapped as *one* tuple-typed param instead of being read as a 2-param list. Fixed by unwrapping a bare `TypeExpr::Tuple` LHS into `params` directly when immediately followed by `=>` (an LHS that's `(A, B)?` — Option-wrapped — is untouched, correctly staying a single param, since by that point its node is `Option`, not `Tuple`). Also correctly handles the zero-param case (`() => Int`) as 0 params instead of one empty-tuple param, as a side effect. 6 new parser tests (2/3/0/1-param cases, the optional-tuple non-split guard, a plain-non-arrow-tuple regression guard). | `crates/parser` |
| 108 | Bug fixed — calling a function-valued local variable never worked in codegen, for any arity | Found while verifying item 99's fix end-to-end: even the always-correctly-parsed single-param case, `fn applyOne(f: Int => Int, a: Int): Int = f(a)`, failed to compile the generated C ("too many arguments to function call, expected 0, have 1"). Every Certo function *value* (not a direct call to a named top-level function) compiles to the single opaque typedef `certo_fn_t` (`crates/codegen/src/ty_to_c.rs` — `typedef void (*certo_fn_t)(void);`, a zero-arg pointer used uniformly regardless of the value's real arity), and `emit_mir.rs`'s call-emission (`Rvalue::Call`/`Terminator::Call`) called it directly with no cast, so the C compiler used `certo_fn_t`'s zero-arg signature for the call regardless of how many arguments were actually passed. A named global function call was never affected (it calls the real, already-correctly-typed C function directly, no `certo_fn_t` involved) — only calling a function held in a local (a parameter, `val`, or closure) was broken. This path was essentially unreachable before item 99's fix: earlier, valid multi-param `(A, B) => C` annotations couldn't even be written, and this exact investigation is what found that the same class of call was *already* broken even for the one arity (1-param) that always parsed correctly — nobody had ever exercised passing-and-calling a function value in this compiler. Fixed with a new `emit_callee` helper in `crates/codegen/src/emit_mir.rs` that, for a call whose callee is a local (not a named global), looks up the local's `Ty::Fn { params, ret }` and casts it to the real C function-pointer signature (e.g. `((int64_t(*)(int64_t,int64_t))f)(a,b)`) before calling — for both a named function passed by reference and an inline lambda value. 3 new codegen tests (2-param and 1-param casts present; a named direct call confirmed *not* cast, since it never needed to be). | `crates/codegen` |
| 109 | Pattern-to-scrutinee type checking — a `match` arm's pattern (and `val` destructuring pattern) is now actually type-checked against what it's matching, instead of just binding fresh, totally unconstrained type variables | Found while scoping item 73 (match exhaustiveness): `match n { "hello" => "a", _ => "b" }` against an `Int` scrutinee produced **no error at all** — `bind_pattern_vars`, the function both `Expr::Match` and `val` destructuring used, was explicitly marked `// simplified — full pattern inference is complex` and the scrutinee's inferred type was outright discarded (`let _ = scrut_ty;`). A real exhaustiveness checker needs to know each pattern's actual implied type to reason about coverage, so this had to be fixed first. New `check_pattern` in `crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs` recursively unifies every pattern kind (literal, tuple, list, constructor, record, plus the transparent `As`/`Or`/`Guard` wrappers) against the expected type, binding variables with their precise inferred types instead of fresh unconstrained ones, and reports real mismatches: a literal pattern of the wrong type, a constructor from an unrelated type (e.g. `Ok(x)` matched against a plain `Int`), or a constructor called with the wrong number of fields (reuses the existing E0204 `ArityMismatch`). Replaces both call sites (`bind_pattern_vars` in `Expr::Match`, `bind_pattern_tys` in `Stmt::Val`) with the one function; `bind_pattern_tys` is now dead and removed. **Known v1 gap, called out in the code:** a named-field sum-variant pattern (`Circle { radius: r }`, as opposed to positional `Circle(r)`) still falls back to fresh-var binding, since variant fields are registered as a positional constructor `Fn` today with no per-field name→type registry to check against (ordinary record-type patterns, e.g. `Order { id, total }`, are unaffected — those go through the existing `record_fields` registry and are fully checked). 10 new tests; verified against every example `.cto` and a real match-heavy program built and run end-to-end with no behavior change. Item 73 (actual exhaustiveness checking) is now unblocked and is the direct next step. | `crates/typeck` |
| 73 | Match exhaustiveness checking (E0213) | New `check_exhaustive` in `crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs`, run after a `match`'s arms are inferred (needs item 109's real pattern typing to know what each arm actually covers). An unguarded wildcard/binding arm anywhere always makes a match exhaustive, checked first regardless of type — a guarded arm (`pat if cond`) never counts as coverage, since the guard could fail at runtime. Beyond that, scope is deliberately split in two: **fully correct** for `Bool` (`true`/`false`), `Option` (`Some`/`None`), `Result` (`Ok`/`Err`), and user-declared sum types (new `TypeEnv.sum_variants: HashMap<type_name, [variant_name]>` registry, populated alongside existing variant-constructor registration) — these have a finite, known set of cases, so missing ones are named exactly (`missing: Triangle`); **requires an explicit catch-all** for everything else (`Int`, `Text`, `Float`, tuples, lists, records, opaque named types) — there's no finite literal/shape set that's ever exhaustive on its own for these, so this isn't "unimplemented," it's the only sound answer. One recognized exception: a tuple pattern whose every element is itself irrefutable (e.g. `(a, b)` alone) is accepted as exhaustive without a wildcard, since a tuple type has exactly one shape — this is checked structurally with no type info needed. The same isn't extended to `Record`/`Constructor` patterns (even though a plain, non-sum record type has the same one-shape property) since telling "the one shape of a record type" apart from "one variant of a sum type" needs the type environment, which the purely-structural `is_catch_all` check doesn't have — documented as a known v1 conservative limitation in the code (a single `Order { id, total } => ...` arm still needs a trailing `_`). Or-patterns (`true | false`) count as covering whatever either branch covers. 20 new tests; verified end-to-end via `certo check` against every example `.cto` (zero regressions) and hand-written non-exhaustive cases for each category (sum type, `Option`, `Bool`), confirmed to fire with accurate `missing: ...` lists and disappear once the missing arms are added. | `crates/typeck`, `crates/cli` |
| 77 | Result combinators — `flatMap`/`mapErr`/`getOrElse`/`recover`/`Result.all`/`Result.allSettled` | New `crates/stdlib/src/result.rs` (`RESULT_C`), registered in `full_c_runtime()` after `Stdlib.Collections` (needs its `CertoList`/`CertoFn1`) and in `seed.rs` (added a `poly3` helper for the 3-type-var combinators); documented in `docs/STDLIB-QUICKREF.md` (see item 107 — this session also deleted the doc-text-only `*_CERTO` constants that item 107 flagged, so `RESULT_CERTO` was never carried forward). **Design decision on call syntax:** the spec shows *both* `result.flatMap(v => ...)` (dot/instance-call) and `db.carts.find(id) |> flatMap(validateCart) |> flatMap(...)` (pipe, bare function names) for the same combinators — but Certo has no true `value.method()` dispatch (only `Type.method(...)` static-qualified calls, resolved by the uppercase-first-letter heuristic added for item 101); the dot-call form in the spec isn't actually achievable. Implemented `flatMap`/`mapErr`/`getOrElse`/`recover` as bare top-level function names instead, exactly matching the spec's own pipe-chain example (`r \|> flatMap(f)` desugars to `flatMap(r, f)`, which reads almost identically to the dot form at the call site). `Result.all`/`Result.allSettled` **are** implemented `Result.`-qualified, matching the working `List.map`-style static-namespace convention, since the spec's own usage for these (`Result.all([r1, r2, r3])`) already matches that pattern. `Result.allSettled` is a deliberate identity function — Certo's `Result` has no separate future/promise to await, so by the time you have a `List<Result<T,E>>` every outcome is already settled; it exists for symmetry with `.all` and to name the "keep every outcome, don't short-circuit" intent at the call site. 14 new tests (6 stdlib registration/C-runtime-presence, 8 full type-check via a `seed_builtins` + `seed_stdlib`-seeded env covering every combinator plus one deliberate type error); verified end-to-end with a real 9-assertion `.cto` program built and run (`flatMap` chaining and short-circuiting, `mapErr` leaving `Ok` untouched, `getOrElse`/`recover` unwrapping, `Result.all` short-circuiting on the first `Err`, `Result.allSettled` preserving both outcomes) — every result matched expectations exactly. | `crates/stdlib` |
| 107 | Deleted the unparseable, unused `*_CERTO` doc-text constants and `certo_stdlib::certo_sources()` | Resolved by deletion rather than by teaching the parser `fn Type.method(...)` syntax: `docs/STDLIB-QUICKREF.md` is now the single, sole reference for the stdlib's Certo-facing API surface, removing the risk of the two drifting apart (which is exactly what had already happened — the `*_CERTO` text was stale/broken pseudo-syntax nobody was keeping in sync). Removed the `pub const {MODULE}_CERTO: &str = ...` block (always the last item in its file) from all 20 `crates/stdlib/src/*.rs` module files, the `certo_sources()` function and every `*_CERTO` re-export from `lib.rs`, the three tests in `tests.rs` that only exercised `certo_sources()`, and the stale reference to it in `effects_seed.rs`'s doc comment. Added a `## Result` section to `STDLIB-QUICKREF.md` first, so the Result combinators (item 77, added the same session) didn't end up completely undocumented once `RESULT_CERTO` was gone — that combinator set had no prior QUICKREF entry at all. Verified zero behavioral impact (these were always dead, doc-only text — never compiled into the runtime or consumed by `certo build`/`certo check`): full workspace build and test suite clean, every example `.cto` still checks exactly as before. | `crates/stdlib`, `docs/STDLIB-QUICKREF.md` |
| 104 | Removed MSVC `cl.exe` from `find_cc`'s compiler candidate list | Resolved by removal rather than by adding MSVC argument-translation support: `cl.exe` was never actually usable anyway (see the original finding — `probe_cc`'s `--version` check always failed for it, and even if detection worked, the compiler invocation is 100% GCC/Clang-flag syntax `cl.exe` doesn't understand), so keeping it in the list only meant it would silently fail detection forever, never doing anything. `find_cc` in `crates/cli/src/main.rs` now only tries `clang`/`gcc`/`cc`, with a comment explaining why `cl` is deliberately excluded. The existing "no C compiler found" error message already only ever named `cc`/`gcc`/`clang`, so no user-facing wording needed to change. Verified `clang` (installed via `winget install LLVM.LLVM` earlier this session) is still detected and used correctly; full workspace test suite clean. If MSVC support is ever wanted for real, it needs the parallel argument-translation path described in the original finding — this item just removes the misleading "supported candidate that does nothing" middle ground. | `crates/cli` |
| 110 | Fixed `crates/resolve` never registering user-declared sum-type variant names; kept it deliberately unwired from `certo build`/`certo check` | Root cause was `resolve_decl.rs`'s `Decl::Type(_) => {}`, which skipped type bodies entirely — sum-type variant names (`Circle`, `Square`, ...) never entered `resolve`'s scope, so `resolve_pattern.rs`'s constructor-pattern lookup flagged any reference to one as `UndefinedIdent`. Fixed in `resolve_module.rs::hoist_decls`: for every `Decl::Type` whose body is `TypeBody::Sum`, each variant name is now hoisted into the top-level scope alongside the type name itself, mirroring how `crates/typeck/src/infer_decl.rs` already registers `env.sum_variants`. Separately investigated whether `certo_resolve` is worth wiring into the real compiler pipeline at all (it currently isn't — `crates/cli` doesn't even depend on it): concluded no for its main check, since undefined-name detection (E0100) is fully redundant with `certo_typeck`'s own `UnboundName` (E0206), which the real pipeline already runs; typeck's `error.rs` even has a comment calling E0206 "belt and suspenders" for exactly this reason. What `resolve` uniquely catches — `DuplicateDefinition`/`AmbiguousImport` (E0101/E0102) and validator rule-graph cycles (E0700-E0702) — doesn't by itself justify running a whole second full-module pass on every compile. Rewrote `crates/resolve/src/lib.rs`'s crate doc to say this explicitly, since it previously read as load-bearing. `certo-lsp` is resolve's one real consumer (live editor diagnostics via `crates/lsp/src/analysis.rs`), and while fixing this, found and fixed a second, more actively-wrong bug there: the LSP called `certo_resolve::resolve(&m)` unseeded, so literally any stdlib call (`print`, `parseInt`, ...) showed up as a false "undefined" squiggle in the editor even though typeck's parallel check (correctly seeded via `certo_stdlib::seed_stdlib`) never complained. Fixed by adding `TypeEnv::names()` (flattens all frames' defined names) and `ScopeChain::seed_extra`/`resolve_module::resolve_seeded`, so the LSP now calls `certo_resolve::resolve_seeded(&m, env.names())` — reusing typeck's own already-seeded name set as the single source of truth instead of hand-maintaining a second stdlib name list. Added 6 new `crates/resolve` tests (sum-type variant match/constructor/unit-variant happy paths, unknown-variant still-E0100, `resolve_seeded` extra-builtin happy path, local-decl-shadows-seeded-builtin). Two `crates/typeck` tests that previously needed a `check_ignoring_resolve` workaround (added when this bug was first found, while implementing match exhaustiveness) now pass with the real `check()` helper, so that workaround was deleted as dead code. Verified end-to-end: full workspace build and test suite clean. While verifying with a real `.cto` program using a named-field sum type, found a distinct, pre-existing, unrelated codegen bug — logged separately as item 111. | `crates/resolve`, `crates/typeck`, `crates/lsp` |
| 83 | Collections: added `distinct`/`partition`/`chunked`/`groupBy` (4 of 7 — `sumBy`/`minBy`/`maxBy` deferred, see item 112) | Implemented in `crates/stdlib/src/collections.rs` (`certo_list_distinct`/`certo_list_partition`/`certo_list_chunked`/`certo_list_group_by`) and registered in `seed.rs` matching the spec's signatures (`docs/Certo_Language_Specification.md`'s stdlib table): `distinct(): List<T>` (pointer-equality dedup, same convention as `List.contains`/Map keys — documented caveat carried into `STDLIB-QUICKREF.md`), `partition(T=>Bool): (List<T>, List<T>)`, `chunked(Int): List<List<T>>`, `groupBy<K>(T=>K): Map<K, List<T>>`. `partition`'s tuple return uses `certo_list_of(2, yes, no)` (a `CertoList*`, matching how the compiler's own `Rvalue::Aggregate(Tuple)` represents tuples) rather than the separate `CertoPair` struct `List.zip` uses internally — found while implementing that `List.zip` + tuple-destructure is *itself* already broken (`certo_list_get_or_panic` reads a `CertoPair*`'s memory as if it were a `CertoList*`), a distinct pre-existing bug not fixed here (not blocking this item, logged for awareness only, not worth a separate line since it's a one-function-pair issue vs. item 112's broader scope). Found and fixed a real prerequisite bug while verifying `groupBy` end-to-end: `ty_to_c(Ty::Map(k,v))` mangled to a per-instantiation typedef name (`certo_map_text_int_t`) that is never actually emitted anywhere, so *any* `Map`-typed value crossing a function boundary — not just `groupBy`, this affected plain `Map.insert`/`Map.get` usage too — failed to compile with "undeclared identifier". Fixed by mapping `Ty::Map` to `void*` like every other opaque heap type (`List`/`Option`/`Result`), since `CertoMap*` is equally opaque regardless of `K`/`V`. `sumBy`/`minBy`/`maxBy` were scoped out after discovering, while designing them, that passing a `Float`-returning lambda into *any* higher-order stdlib function (`List.map` included) already silently produces garbage — a deeper pre-existing lambda-calling-convention bug (see item 112) that these three would immediately have inherited, since they need exactly that shape (`T => Float`). Verified end-to-end with real `.cto` programs (`distinct`/`partition`/`chunked` on `List<Int>`, `groupBy` on both `Int` keys — correct — and `Text` keys, which reproduces Map's own already-documented pointer-equality limitation, not a new bug). Added 6 `crates/stdlib` tests (registration, polymorphism, full type-check including a rejection case) and 2 `crates/codegen` tests (call-site emission, the `Ty::Map` fix). | 111 | Fixed named-field sum-type variants: struct field access mismatch (compile error) and, once that compiled, a silent `Float` payload truncation | Three separate, layered bugs surfaced building up from the original C compile error (`no member named 'f0'`) to a fully correct fix — each only became visible once the previous one was fixed. **(1) Struct field name mismatch:** `emit_module.rs`'s struct emission already used a variant field's real declared name when present (`Circle(radius: Float)` → `.circle.radius`), falling back to `f{i}` only for positional fields — but MIR's match-arm lowering (`mir/src/lower.rs`) always emitted the positional fallback unconditionally, so named-field variants didn't compile at all. Fixed by threading the real field names through: `HirPat::Constructor` gained a `field_names: Vec<String>` computed once in HIR lowering (`Cx::variant_field_names`, populated the same way `emit_module.rs` computes them) and consumed by MIR instead of hardcoding `f{i}`. **(2) Payload type truncation:** once it compiled, `Circle(r) => r` for a `Float` field printed `2` instead of `2.5` — the pattern-bound local `r` was declared with the `Ty::Error` placeholder (codegen maps that to `int64_t`), so the C assignment from the real `double` union member silently truncated on read. Root cause: `HirPat::Bind`-producing patterns never registered a type in `Cx::local_types` at all (only `val` bindings did, despite a comment on that code claiming it was "needed for `match q { Some(x) => … }`" — it wasn't actually wired up for any match pattern). Fixed by threading the scrutinee's type through `lower_pat` (new `scrut_ty: &Ty` parameter) so every bind site — plain `Pattern::Ident`, tuple elements, and constructor fields (built-in `Some`/`Ok`/`Err` payload types read off `scrut_ty`'s `Ty::Option`/`Ty::Result`, user sum-type fields off the same `variant_field_types` table added for fix (1)) — registers its real type in `cx.local_types`, so later references via `Expr::Path` resolve correctly instead of defaulting to `Ty::Error`. **(3) Match/if join-type truncation for computed arm bodies:** fixing (2) still wasn't enough once an arm body was a computed expression (`Circle(r) => r * r * 3.14159`) rather than a bare identifier — `HirExprKind::BinOp` always self-types as `Ty::Error` at the HIR level by design (a comment on that code already explains MIR recovers the real type from operands via `infer_operand_ty`), but `Expr::Match`'s own inferred type only ever looked at whether an arm's *body* had a non-`Error` HIR type, which a `BinOp` body never does — so the match's join-local defaulted to `int64_t` regardless of fix (2). Fixed in `mir/lower.rs` by patching the join local's type after lowering each arm's operand, via the same `infer_operand_ty` idiom `lower_fn` already uses for return-type inference; applied identically to `if`/`else`, which has the exact same gap. Added 6 new `crates/codegen` tests (named-field access, multi-field position mapping, `Float` field bound as `double` not truncated, computed match-arm bodies returning `double`, computed if-branch bodies returning `double`) plus verified end-to-end with real `.cto` programs (`certo run`) at each stage — all three bugs reproduced and were fixed independently, confirmed via the actual printed output (`2.5`/`7.25`/`12.5664`/`9`, not `2`/`7`/`12`/`9`). Full workspace build and test suite clean. | `crates/hir`, `crates/mir`, `crates/codegen` |
| 112 | Fixed the `Float`-callback calling-convention mismatch for `List.map`/`filter`/`find`/`any`/`all`/`groupBy` | A lambda passed directly to these stdlib higher-order functions lifts to its own real native C signature (e.g. `double __lam_main_0(double x)`), but their C runtime parameter is the generic `CertoFn1`/`CertoPred` pointer type (`void* (*)(void*)`) — calling one through the other silently misreads the wrong register (XMM0 vs RAX) on real x86-64/x64 ABIs, corrupting `Float` values (`Int`/`Bool`/`Text` callbacks happened to already work, since those share the general-purpose-register convention with `void*`). Fixed narrowly rather than via a full uniform-ABI redesign (considered and rejected as touching item 108's `emit_callee` and every existing HOF call site for uncertain benefit): a lambda literal passed *directly* as the callback to one of a hardcoded allowlist (`BOXED_ABI_CALLEES` in `mir/src/lower.rs`) is lifted via a new `lower_lambda_boxed`, using two new MIR ops (`Rvalue::Box`/`Rvalue::Unbox`, reusing the existing `box_value`/`unbox_value` codegen text-generation already used for tuple/list/Option `Float` bit-preservation) to unbox each param on entry and box the return on exit. Lambda params are almost always unannotated, so the real param type is recovered from the call site's known scrutinee-list element type rather than the lambda's own (missing) annotation. Explicitly out of scope: `List.sort`'s two-argument `Int`-returning comparator (different C ABI shape) and user-defined higher-order functions (already internally consistent via item 108, confirmed not regressed). Initially verified only in isolation (direct C inspection) because the end-to-end case was blocked by a second, independent bug — see item 113, whose fix closed the loop; `List.map`/`groupBy` on `List<Float>` now produce correct results end-to-end (`[1.1,2.2,3.3].map(x => x*1.5)` → `1.65, 3.3, 4.95`, confirmed via a real `.cto` program). 3 `crates/codegen` tests. | `crates/mir`, `crates/codegen`, `crates/llvm` |
| 113 | HIR now recovers the return type of the stdlib functions most likely to carry `Float` payloads through generic containers, instead of defaulting every stdlib call to `Ty::Error` | Root cause: `hir/src/lower.rs`'s `Expr::App` only populated `cx.fn_ret_types` for user-declared `Decl::Fn`/`Decl::Impl` — every stdlib call's `HirExprKind::Call` got `ty: Ty::Error`, so nothing downstream (a `val` binding, a `for` loop, `cx.local_types`'s explicit Ty::Error-skipping guard) ever learned it was handling a `Float`. Found while trying to verify item 112 end-to-end: `List.map([1.0,2.0,3.0], (x) => x*2.0)` still printed garbage even after that fix, because `val ys = List.map(...)` itself stayed untyped. **Deliberately not a real unification engine** — extended the small, pre-existing, hand-maintained `generic_container_ret` helper (which already covered `List.get`/`first`/`last`/`find` → `Option<elem>`) with mechanically-derivable rules for functions where the return type follows structurally from argument types alone: `List.getOrPanic` (bare `elem`), `filter`/`sort`/`reverse`/`distinct`/`slice`/`concat`/`push` (same list type), `partition` (tuple of two), `chunked` (`List<List<T>>`), `Map.get`/`remove`/`insert`/`keys`/`values`. **`List.map`/`groupBy` needed more** — their return type depends on the *callback's* return type, which HIR can't know for an unannotated lambda without inference. Closed this with two small, targeted additions: (1) a `binop_result_ty` at the HIR level (mirroring the one MIR already had) so `x * 2.0`'s own type resolves to `Float` instead of unconditionally `Ty::Error`; (2) a new `lower_lambda_with_param_hint`, used only for `List.map`'s/`groupBy`'s callback argument, that pre-seeds the lambda param's type from the already-lowered scrutinee list's element type (falling back to an explicit param annotation if present) *before* lowering the body — so `x`'s references inside the callback resolve correctly, letting (1) do the rest. `generic_container_ret` then reads the callback body's now-known type off the lowered lambda. A third gap surfaced verifying this: `List.getOrPanic` always returns a raw `void*` at the C level regardless of the now-correct logical type HIR reports, so when that logical type is `Float`/`Decimal` the raw pointer must be unboxed (`Rvalue::Unbox`, new in item 112) rather than assigned straight into a `double` local — fixed in `mir/lower.rs`'s `HirExprKind::Call` lowering (a small, explicit `RAW_RETURN_CALLEES` allowlist, currently just `List.getOrPanic` — the only *new* function in this item's scope returning a bare, non-container-wrapped element type; existing `Option`/`List`/`Map`/`Tuple`-returning functions don't need it since those always compile to `void*` regardless of contents). Verified end-to-end with real `.cto` programs: `List.map`/`getOrPanic`/`for`-loop-iteration on `List<Float>` all produce exactly correct results now (previously silently wrong). Explicitly still out of scope: most other stdlib functions (`Text`/`DateTime`/`Json`/etc.) still default to `Ty::Error`, and nothing here does real generic instantiation — this is a curated, incremental table matching the codebase's existing hand-maintained-metadata convention (`seed.rs`, `effects_seed.rs`), not a general fix. 9 new tests (7 `crates/hir`, 2 `crates/codegen`). | 74 | `priv` constructors (smart-constructor pattern) — `type X = priv X(...)` | Parses a single-constructor "newtype" (`TypeBody::Sum` with exactly one variant sharing the type's own name, e.g. `type Email = priv Email(Text)`) via the existing `parse_sum_variants_no_leading_bar`, gated on a new `TypeDecl.is_priv_ctor: bool` set when `priv` precedes the constructor. Enforced with a new typeck pass (`check_priv_ctors` in `infer_decl.rs`, mirroring the existing `check_ffi_unsafe` AST-walk pattern): the raw constructor (`Email(...)`) may only be called from within an `impl Email { ... }` block for the same type — everywhere else (top-level functions, `val`/`var` initializers, `impl` blocks for *other* types) is a new E0214, matching the spec's own example exactly (`Email.new` calling `Email(raw)` internally is fine; a bare top-level `Email("bad")` is rejected). Verifying this end-to-end surfaced and fixed two real, distinct, pre-existing bugs that only a variant-named-identically-to-its-own-type could ever trigger (never possible before `priv`, since no other Certo feature produces that shape): (1) `certo_resolve`'s hoisting (item 110) registered a sum type's variant names *and* separately its own type name — for a normal sum type these never collide, but for `priv X = X(...)` they're the same string, so resolve spuriously reported `DuplicateDefinition`; fixed by skipping a variant's hoist when it equals its own type's name. (2) A genuine typeck bug, unrelated to resolve: `type_expr_to_ty`'s type-parameter-in-scope lookup (`ctx.env.lookup(name)`, meant only to resolve a generic `T`/`R` bound to a fresh `Ty::Var`) matched *any* value defined under that name, including a sum-type constructor — so a function annotated `fn f(): Email = ...` had its *own return-type annotation* incorrectly resolved to the constructor's `Fn` type instead of the plain nominal `Email`, producing a spurious E0200 mismatch alongside the correct E0214. Fixed by restricting that fallback to only fire when the found binding is actually a `Ty::Var` (a real type parameter), not any arbitrary value. Along the way, also found and fixed a third, broader, pre-existing bug in `crates/hir` (same family as items 111-113): `Expr::App`'s return-type lookup only ever tried the call's *short* name (`"new"`) in `cx.fn_ret_types`, but `impl X { fn m(...) }` methods are hoisted under their *qualified* name (`"X.m"`) — so **every** `Type.method(...)` call's return type silently defaulted to `Ty::Error` in HIR, not just `priv`-constructor factories. Fixed by trying the full qualified path first. Verified end-to-end with real `.cto` programs: the spec's own rejection case (`Email("bad")` outside `impl`) and an `Option`-based smart-constructor (`Email.new` returning `Some`/`None`, called and pattern-matched successfully) both work correctly. **Known residual gap, not fixed here** (out of scope, logged separately as item 114): the spec's own idiomatic `Result<Email, ValidationError>` factory form doesn't compile — a distinct, pre-existing bug where `Result`'s `Ok`/`Err` C runtime assumes a pointer-sized payload and can't hold a real multi-byte struct value (confirmed unrelated to `priv`: the same failure reproduces for any struct-shaped payload in `Ok(...)`, whenever something forces the payload's real type to be known rather than silently defaulting to `Ty::Error`/`int64_t`). 9 new tests (3 `crates/parser`, 5 `crates/typeck`, 1 `crates/resolve` fix covered by existing suite). | 114 | Fixed `Ok(v)`/`Err(e)` and `e?` for struct-shaped payloads (records, payload-carrying sum types, `Decimal`, `UUID`) | `certo_ok`/`certo_err`'s C runtime is a single pointer-sized `intptr_t` slot; a struct-shaped payload (passed *by value* per the existing tagged-union/record C representation) doesn't fit and previously only had a `Float`-specific bit-cast special case (from items 111/112). Fixed by adding a new `needs_result_box(ty)` predicate (`mir/src/lower.rs`) — true for `Decimal`/`UUID` (themselves multi-field C structs) and any other `Ty::Named` not in the small hardcoded opaque-handle list (`HttpRequest`/`Bytes`/`DbResult`/etc., which already fit as `int64_t`) — and reusing `Option`'s existing `Rvalue::BoxSome`/`UnboxSome` heap-boxing mechanism for both `Ok`/`Err` construction and match-arm extraction, exactly mirroring how `Some(v)` already handles arbitrary-sized payloads correctly. Deliberately conservative: a nullary-enum `Named` type would technically already fit unboxed, but boxing it anyway is harmless (one extra small allocation) — the point is *never* passing a real struct where the runtime expects a pointer-sized int. Verifying this surfaced a **second, independent** bug in the exact same area: the `?` (Try) desugar has its own separate `__result_unwrap` call site in `mir/src/lower.rs` that never got item 111/112's `Float` fix at all, and its root cause was even further upstream — `Expr::Try`'s own HIR type was unconditionally `Ty::Error` (`hir/src/lower.rs`), so `e?` on a `Result<Float, E>` silently truncated too, independent of anything in this item's main fix. Fixed by extracting the Ok-payload type from the inner expression's `Ty::Result(t, _)`, and refactoring the match-arm and `?`-desugar unwrap paths to share one `unwrap_result_into` helper so both get the same Float/struct/plain handling instead of two independently-maintained copies. Verified end-to-end with real `.cto` programs: the spec's own `Email.new` factory returning `Result<Email, ValidationError>` (the original motivating case for this item) now compiles and runs correctly end-to-end, including via `Email.new(...)?`; `Result<Decimal, E>` and `Result<UUID, E>` construction/matching verified likewise (isolated from an unrelated, already-documented item-113-shaped gap: `Decimal.fromInt`/`.add`'s own stdlib return type isn't threaded into HIR yet, since item 113 only extended that table for `List`/`Map`). | 116 | Fixed: any *parameterized* type used as a record/sum-type field (built-in generics: `List<Int>`, `Map<K,V>`, explicit `Option<T>`/`Result<A,B>`, `T?`) previously failed to compile at all | Started as item 78's `Secret<T>` prerequisite (user-defined generic erasure); investigating it surfaced a materially bigger, previously-undiscovered bug first: `emit_module.rs`'s struct/constructor-emission pre-pass (`ast_ty_to_c_str`, which runs on the raw AST `TypeExpr` *before* typeck/HIR/MIR, so it can't consult the resolved `Ty`) only ever looked at a `TypeExpr::Named`'s bare *name*, silently ignoring its generic `args` entirely — so `type Order = { items: List<Int> }` emitted a literal, undeclared `List items;` field (confirmed via a minimal repro, not just theorized). The same function's `TypeExpr::Option{..}` branch had an independent, adjacent bug: it mapped `T?` fields to `certo_option_t` — the *external*, ABI-stable struct `crates/ffi`'s header generator deliberately uses for its public API — instead of the *internal* nullable-pointer representation (`ty_to_c(Ty::Option(_)) => "void*"`) every other read/write of an Option value in the codebase actually assumes; also confirmed broken via a real repro (`assigning to 'int64_t' from incompatible type 'certo_option_t'`). Fixed by making any `Named` with non-empty `args` map to `"void*"` (matching how every generic value is *already* represented everywhere else in codegen — no new boxing needed, since a `List<Int>` value already *is* a `CertoList*` pointer at runtime, so a straight pointer store/load into a now-`void*`-typed field just works), and by correcting the `Option`/non-empty-`Tuple` branches to `"void*"` to match their real internal representation. **Does not include user-defined generic type erasure** (`type Box<T> = { value: T }`, needed for `Secret<T>`/`Permission<T>`) — investigating that surfaced a deeper, separate blocker: HIR never tracks a generic type's concrete instantiation argument at all, so construction/field-access boxing alone isn't sufficient — split out as a sharper item 119 (Pending), since the fix needed is real new instantiation-tracking machinery, not just wiring up `BoxSome`/`UnboxSome` at a couple of sites. Verified end-to-end with real `.cto` programs via `certo.exe`: a record field typed `List<Int>` (construct + `List.len` read), a record field typed `Text?` (`Some`/`None` construct + match, including the previously-uncaught `None` case), a record field typed `Map<Text,Int>`, and a sum-type variant payload typed `List<Int>` (`Items([1,2,3])`, matched and read) all now compile and produce correct results. 5 new `crates/codegen` tests. Full workspace test suite clean. Also surfaced, but explicitly out of scope and *not* fixed here: `Map.fromList`/`Map.get` for `Text` keys returns `None` for a key that should be present — reproduces identically with zero record fields involved, so it's an unrelated, pre-existing `crates/stdlib` bug, not a regression from this item's changes. | `crates/codegen` |
| 119 | Generic record types (construction + field access) and direct-scope generic sum-type variants (construction + pattern match) now correctly heap-box/unbox a bare type-param field | Real instantiation-tracking, not just wiring up boxing at existing sites (per item 116's sharper scoping) — a genuinely new subsystem. **HIR**: new `record_field_types`/`variant_field_types` tables (declared field types, `Ty::Var(0)` marking a bare type-param field) exposed via `HirModule` and threaded into `crates/mir`'s `Builder`/`lower_fn` (new parameters — `lower_fn`'s signature changed, all 3 call sites updated). A record literal (`Box { value: 5 }`) recovers its concrete instantiation argument from the first `Ty::Var(0)` field's actual value type and tags the result `Ty::Named{"Box", args:[Ty::Int]}` instead of always-empty `args` — same recovery for a direct sum-type variant constructor call (`Items(42)`). Field access (`b.value`) and match-arm payload binding (`Items(v) => ...`) substitute a `Ty::Var(0)` declared field type back to the concrete type carried on the base/scrutinee's own `args`. **MIR**: record construction and known-variant constructor calls heap-box any `Ty::Var(_)`-declared argument via the existing `Rvalue::BoxSome` (items 111/114's mechanism); field reads and match-arm bindings unbox via `Rvalue::UnboxSome` when the declared type was `Ty::Var(_)`, using the substituted concrete type. **Codegen**: fixed the struct-layout pre-pass (`ast_ty_to_c_str`, item 116's target) to *also* erase a bare type-param field name (not just non-empty generic args) to `void*` — needs the enclosing type's own `type_params` threaded in (`field_c_ty`), since the parser never actually constructs `TypeExpr::Param` (verified dead code); and fixed `ty_to_c`'s `Ty::Named` non-empty-args branch, previously dead/untested code that produced a bogus mangled-per-instantiation type name (`Box_int_t`) — a user generic type isn't monomorphized, so the C type is always the bare struct name regardless of `args`. **Found and fixed two more real, independent bugs along the way**: HIR's own `Decl::Impl` lowering (both its pre-registration pass and its actual body-lowering pass — two separate code paths) only ever scoped a method's own `<U>` type params, never the enclosing `impl<T> { ... }` block's `<T>` — the exact bug item 115 already fixed on the *typeck* side, but HIR runs its own independent lowering pass that never got the fix, so `impl<T> Secret { fn wrap(v: T): Secret<T> = ... }` emitted the literal, undeclared C type name `T` for the parameter. | 81 | Enforce `parallel(timeout:)` — the timeout was parsed but silently dropped before typeck, HIR, or codegen ever saw it again | Semantics weren't defined anywhere (spec shows the syntax, never says what happens on expiry) — user decision: **panic** with a clear message, keeping `parallel(timeout: ...) { ... }`'s type identical to plain `parallel { ... }` (still a tuple, no new `Result`/error-type machinery). **typeck**: `timeout:` is now actually constrained to `Duration` (previously not type-checked at all — only walked by two unrelated AST visitors for FFI/privacy checks). **HIR**: `Expr::Parallel` with a timeout computes one shared absolute deadline (`certo_monotonic_millis() + Duration.toSeconds(timeout)*1000`) into a fresh local *before* spawning any tasks, and every task's join becomes a new `HirExprKind::AwaitTimed { task, deadline }` referencing that same local, instead of plain `Await` — so the deadline is an overall budget across the whole block, not a fresh timeout per task (each join's remaining budget is computed fresh as `deadline - now()`, correctly deducting time already spent waiting on earlier tasks). **MIR**: new `Rvalue::JoinTimed { task, deadline, ret_ty }`. **Codegen/runtime**: new cross-platform `__certo_thread_join_timed` — Windows uses `WaitForSingleObject`'s native millisecond timeout directly on the existing thread handle (no extra state needed); POSIX has no portable timed `pthread_join` (`pthread_timedjoin_np` is a Linux-only glibc extension), so every task's shared context struct now always carries a `sem_t` the worker posts on completion (harmless no-op overhead for plain untimed `spawn`/`parallel {}`, which keep using the existing unbounded `__certo_thread_join`), waited on via `sem_timedwait` with an absolute deadline. On timeout the thread/handle/semaphore are deliberately leaked (no cancellation mechanism exists, and the caller panics immediately after — the process is about to exit). Added `sleep(ms: Int): Unit [io]` (new `certo_sleep`, cross-platform `Sleep`/`nanosleep`) as the test harness for this item — didn't exist at all before, and is a generally useful primitive regardless. Verified end-to-end with real `.cto` programs via `certo.exe`: a `parallel(timeout: Duration.seconds(5))` block with two 50ms tasks completes normally with the correct summed result; the same shape with one task sleeping 3s under a 1s timeout panics with `"parallel(timeout: ...) block exceeded its timeout"` (confirmed same exit-code convention as every other `certo_panic` in the codebase, not a shell-level failure); plain `parallel {}` (no timeout) is unaffected and still uses the original unbounded join path. 4 new `crates/codegen` tests, 2 new `crates/typeck` tests, 1 new `crates/stdlib` test. | 125 | `test --filter`, `check --strict`/`--explain`, `run --watch` (real rebuild-and-rerun) | Split from item 92, which bundled these with `run --port` and `test --coverage` — see below for why those two are handled differently, not just deferred wholesale. **`test --filter <substring>`**: the underlying mechanism (`RunOptions.filter`, `crates/testrunner/src/run.rs`) already existed and was already tested — it was simply never wired from `cmd_test`'s argument parsing in `crates/cli/src/main.rs` (a *separate*, lesser-known standalone `certo-test` binary, `crates/testrunner/src/main.rs`, already had it; the primary `certo test` subcommand did not). Now parses `--filter <substring>` and passes it through. **`check --strict`**: reuses the existing `cmd_lint::lint_hir` pass (previously only reachable via the separate `certo lint` subcommand) — after `run_typeck` passes, `--strict` also runs lint and exits 1 if any warnings are found, rather than inventing a second, parallel warning system. **`check --explain`**: `docs/ERROR-REFERENCE.md` already had a full per-code write-up for every diagnostic (`### E0200  Type mismatch`, cause, fix table, examples) — embedded into the `certo` binary via `include_str!` (works regardless of cwd or whether the repo is even present at runtime) and looked up by code (`explain_code`, a plain substring/heading scan, not a real Markdown parser — sufficient given the file's own consistent `### CODE  Title` / `---`-separated structure). `run_typeck` gained an `explain: bool` parameter threaded through its ~5 error-and-exit points (type/trait/effect/schema/schema-sync errors); the two other call sites (`cmd_build`, `cmd_bench`) go through an unchanged `run_typeck` wrapper that always passes `false`, so this is check-only. **`run --watch`**: found and fixed a real bug while implementing this, not just an unwired flag — `--watch` previously flowed straight through into `cmd_build`'s own watch handling (already implemented, for `certo build`), whose loop only ever rebuilds; it never executes the compiled binary. `certo run --watch` would therefore have rebuilt forever without the program running even once. Fixed the same way `cmd_build`'s own `--watch` works (not by refactoring `cmd_build` to be non-exiting): `cmd_run` now intercepts `--watch`/`-w` before the one-shot path, and re-invokes itself as a `certo run <args without --watch>` subprocess per file change via the existing `cmd_watch::watch_loop`, so both the rebuild *and* the re-run happen together each tick, with the program's real stdout/stderr/exit code passed through. **`run --port`: not implemented, deliberately** — `certo run app.cto -- --port 8080` already reaches the program as an ordinary argument (the `--` separator this command already supports), which the program reads via `arg(n)`/`getEnv`; a dedicated `--port` flag would just be a narrower, special-cased alias for a mechanism that already exists and already works for any flag, not a new capability. Verified end-to-end via the real `certo.exe`: `--filter` narrows a 3-test file to the one matching test; `--strict` exits 1 with the two expected L001/L002 lint warnings on code that plain `check` passes; `--explain` prints the exact E0200 reference section right after a real type error; `--watch` was run live — printed "version 1", the source file was edited to print "version 2", and the watch loop rebuilt and reran automatically within one poll interval, printing "version 2" with no manual restart. 5 new `crates/cli` tests (`explain_code`'s markdown-section boundary handling, including the tricky case of the *last* entry in a category not bleeding into the next `##` header). Full workspace test suite clean. See item 126 for `test --coverage`, deferred for a real reason (source-mapping gap), not bundled in here as a partial slice. | `crates/cli` |
| 123 | Bug fixed — `certo test`'s harness preamble was missing the Windows `_USE_MATH_DEFINES`/`WIN32_LEAN_AND_MEAN`/winsock-before-windows.h setup `certo build` already had, so any test file pulling in `Stdlib.Math` (even indirectly, since `full_c_runtime()` always includes it) failed to compile at all with `use of undeclared identifier 'M_PI'` | Found while trying to verify item 86 end-to-end — `certo test`/`certo-test.exe` couldn't compile *any* test file on Windows, unrelated to property testing itself. `crates/testrunner/src/harness.rs`'s preamble was a smaller, out-of-sync copy of `crates/cli/src/main.rs`'s own preamble (missing `#ifdef _WIN32 ... #endif`'s `_USE_MATH_DEFINES` and winsock2.h-before-windows.h guards). | 86 | True property-based testing — `property "name"(params...) { body }` now generates and shrinks random inputs, not just running the body once | `PropertyDecl` gained an optional typed parameter list (`crates/ast/src/decl.rs`, parsed in `crates/parser/src/parse_decl.rs` by reusing `parse_fn_param`; bound into scope in `crates/resolve/src/resolve_decl.rs` like an ordinary function's params; printed by `crates/fmt`) — `property "name" { .. }` with no parens still parses and still just runs once, unchanged. **Design decision, not specified by the spec** (which only shows an aspirational `forAll(...)` call that doesn't exist in the codebase, no concrete syntax): typed property *parameters themselves* are the generation surface, avoiding the need for a separate higher-order `forAll` construct entirely — `property "commutes"(x: Int, y: Int) { .. }` *is* the `forAll`. Generation and shrinking live entirely on the **Rust side**, in a new `crates/testrunner/src/gen.rs` — never inside the compiled test binary — deliberately avoiding the alternative design of adding a C-side RNG plus making `certo_panic` catchable (e.g. via `setjmp`/`longjmp`) so a single process could loop through cases itself: that would have meant conditionally changing `certo_panic`'s fatal `abort()` behavior, used by every panic in the entire codebase, for every test run. Instead, the *existing* one-subprocess-per-test-name harness (`crates/testrunner/src/harness.rs`/`run.rs`, pre-existing architecture, unmodified in kind) is reused as-is: the synthesized wrapper `FnDecl` now takes the property's real typed params (ordinary codegen, no new HIR/MIR work needed), the generated `main()` dispatch decodes `argv[2..]` into the right C types per param (`atoll`/`atof`/`strcmp(...,"true")`/raw pointer for `Int`/`Float`/`Bool`/`Text` respectively) and calls the function once, and a failing case is exactly the same signal every other test already used — a nonzero exit code. The Rust-side driver (`run.rs`) generates 100 cases by default (`CERTO_TEST_CASES` to override) from a `splitmix64` PRNG seeded from `CERTO_TEST_SEED` or the current time (no external `rand` dependency — self-contained, deterministic given a seed), spawning the already-compiled binary once per case with the generated values encoded as plain argv strings (no shell involved, so arbitrary generated `Text` needs no escaping). On the first failing case, a greedy shrink loop (`gen::shrink`, bounded to 200 subprocess spawns) repeatedly tries smaller per-parameter candidates — toward `0`/`0.0`/`false`/`""` — re-spawning the binary and checking its exit code each time, sweeping every parameter until a full sweep makes no further progress. The failure report shows the shrunk counterexample, the case index, and `CERTO_TEST_SEED=... CERTO_TEST_CASES=...` to reproduce deterministically (case count must match — the generated "size" scaling is a function of both `case_idx` and total `cases`). **Scope, deliberately closed for this pass**: generation supports `Int`, `Float`, `Bool`, `Text` only — an unsupported parameter type (`List<Int>`, a record, ...) is a clear build-time error naming the property and the offending parameter (`crates/testrunner/src/error.rs`'s new `UnsupportedPropertyParamType`), never a silent no-op or partial feature; `List<T>`/record/sum-type generation is real, well-scoped future work (the dispatch is a plain `match` on `GenType`, trivially extended) — see item 124, unlike e.g. HKT (item 76) this isn't a design-unknown, just unwritten. Verified end-to-end via the real `certo-test.exe` binary: a true property (`x + y == y + x`) passes all 100 generated `Int` cases; a false one (`x < 50`) fails and shrinks to the *exact* boundary `x = 50`; the same seed reproduces the identical failure on a second run; a `Text` property finds and reports a real generated string counterexample; a `Bool` property passes; a zero-param property still runs exactly once (backward compatible); an unsupported-type property produces the clear build error through the actual CLI, not just in a unit test. 2 new `crates/parser` tests, 4 new `crates/testrunner/src/harness.rs` tests, 11 new `crates/testrunner/src/gen.rs` tests (deterministic-given-seed generation, magnitude bounds, and — most importantly — that `shrink` actually converges to a genuinely minimal counterexample for a threshold predicate, not just that it runs). Full workspace test suite clean. | `crates/ast`, `crates/parser`, `crates/resolve`, `crates/fmt`, `crates/testrunner` |
| 80 | `Channel<T>` — a thread-safe, capacity-bounded queue for communicating between `spawn`ed tasks | Split from the original item 80 (`Channel<T>`, `withTimeout`, supervised `spawn`/`every()`) — the other two need real task-cancellation infrastructure the runtime doesn't have at all (spawned threads always run to completion once started), a materially different and larger problem; see item 122 for that design sketch. `Channel<T>` itself has no such dependency — it's a blocking multi-producer/multi-consumer queue backed by a mutex + two condition variables (not-full/not-empty), the same cross-platform split (Win32 `CRITICAL_SECTION`+`CONDITION_VARIABLE` vs POSIX `pthread_mutex_t`+`pthread_cond_t`) as the existing `__certo_thread_*` shim, new in `crates/stdlib/src/channel.rs`. Registered as `Ty::Named{name:"Channel", args:[T]}` — not a new hardcoded `Ty` variant like `Map`/`List` — following the same opaque-`void*`-regardless-of-args pattern as `Map<K,V>` in `ty_to_c.rs` (checked before the generic `Ty::Named` catch-all, which assumes a real per-type C struct exists). `T` is stored as an untyped `void*` slot, the same convention as `List<T>`/`Map<K,V>` (and its same known, pre-existing, undocumented-here limitation for non-pointer-sized structs). API: `Channel.new(capacity: Int): Channel<T>`, `Channel.send`/`Channel.receive` (both block — full/empty respectively), `Channel.tryReceive` (never blocks, `None` if empty right now), `Channel.close` (idempotent, wakes all waiters), `Channel.isClosed`. Two semantics decisions, not specified anywhere (spec shows only `Channel<Order>(capacity: 100)` construction, no method behavior): **`receive` returns `Option<T>`**, not a panic-based sentinel — `None` only once the channel is *both* closed and fully drained (already-buffered items are still delivered after `close`), following the same illegal-states-unrepresentable idiom the rest of the stdlib uses (`Map.get`, `List.first`, ...) rather than inventing a new one; **`send` on an already-closed channel panics** — the same convention as Go's channels, since a closed channel means "no more sends will happen" and silently swallowing or blocking forever are both worse than failing at the point of misuse. Verified end-to-end with real `.cto` programs via `certo.exe`: a producer/consumer pair on two spawned threads with a smaller-than-total-items capacity (forcing the producer to actually block) correctly sums 0+1+2+3+4=10; `Channel.send` on a closed channel panics with `"send on closed channel"`; `Channel.tryReceive` returns `None` immediately on an empty channel and `Some(item)` once one is sent, without blocking; `Channel.isClosed` correctly reflects state before/after `close`. Confirmed `expr.method(...)` dot-call syntax doesn't apply to Channel any more than it does to `Map`/`List` — qualified `Type.method(value, ...)` is the only supported call form for any stdlib type, generic or not; not a Channel-specific gap. 5 new `crates/stdlib` tests, 1 new `crates/codegen` test. Full workspace test suite clean. | `crates/stdlib`, `crates/typeck`, `crates/codegen` |
| 75 | `Float32` and `Char` — new primitive types (`Decimal(p,s)` split out — see item 128) | `Ty::Float32`/`Ty::Char` added to the core type system, mirroring exactly how `Int8`/`Int16`/`Int32`/`UInt` (item 64) were added: new `Ty` variants, exact-match-only `unify` arms (no coercion with `Float`/`Text`/`Int` respectively — confirmed via tests), `type_expr_to_ty` parsing in both `crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs` and its duplicate copy in `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`, C-type mapping in `crates/codegen/src/ty_to_c.rs` (`Float32 → float`, `Char → char`) and the experimental LLVM backend (`crates/llvm/src/ty.rs`, `f32`/`i8`). `Float32`/`Char` were already reserved as builtin names in `crates/resolve/src/scope.rs` from the start — never wired to anything until now. **Found a real, more consequential problem before this could actually work**: `Int8`/`Int16`/`Int32`/`UInt` (item 64) turned out to have *no way to construct a value of those types at all* — no literal syntax, and zero conversion functions anywhere in `seed.rs` — making them types you could declare but never populate; confirmed directly (`val x: Int8 = 5` is a real type error, `Int`'s literal type, not `Int8`'s). Rather than repeat that mistake, `Float32`/`Char` both ship with real construction/conversion functions: `intToFloat32`/`floatToFloat32`/`float32ToFloat`/`float32ToInt`/`float32ToText`, and for `Char` — `Text.charAt(s, i): Char?` (bounds-checked, and Text had *no* char-level access at all before this), `Char.toText`/`toInt`/`fromInt`, plus `isDigit`/`isAlpha`/`isUpperCase`/`isLowerCase`/`isWhitespace`/`toUpperCase`/`toLowerCase` (ASCII-only via existing `<ctype.h>`, consistent with `Text.toUpper`/`toLower`'s existing byte-oriented, non-Unicode convention — not a new limitation). **Found and fixed a second, deeper, genuinely pre-existing bug while verifying Float32 round-trips**: `crates/hir/src/lower.rs` runs its own separate, typeck-independent type inference, and its stdlib-return-type lookup (`stdlib_ret_type`) only ever knew about `parseInt`/`parseFloat`/`parseDecimal` — every other stdlib call's result silently fell back to `Ty::Error` → `int64_t` in codegen. For pointer-sized/int64-compatible types this is harmless (bits round-trip through `int64_t` unchanged), but for `Float`/`Float32` it's a real, silent value-truncation bug: confirmed with a minimal repro completely unrelated to this item — `val f = intToFloat(3); val g = f / 2.0; println(floatToText(g))` prints `1`, not `1.5`, because `f`'s local gets wrongly declared `int64_t` and C's implicit double→int64 conversion truncates it before the division even happens (division then runs as truncating integer division). Fixed narrowly for this item's own new functions only (added `stdlib_ret_type`/new `qualified_stdlib_ret_type` entries, the latter keyed by full `Type.method` path to avoid short-name collisions like a bare `"toText"`) — confirmed the fix works (`floatToFloat32(2.5)` now correctly prints `2.5`, generated C locals are correctly declared `float`/`double` instead of `int64_t`). The pre-existing `intToFloat`/`floatToInt` truncation bug itself, and a full audit of `seed.rs` for other functions with the same gap, is **not** fixed here — flagged as a separate background task, since item 75 didn't introduce it and fixing it properly needs its own scope (ideally deriving HIR's return-type table mechanically from `certo_stdlib::seed_stdlib` instead of hand-listing function names forever, the same architectural question, not a one-line patch). No Char literal syntax (`'a'`) yet — construction goes through `Text.charAt`/`Char.fromInt` instead, deliberately, matching this item's own "real functions over partial literal syntax" standard; a natural, small future addition, not a design-sketch-scale gap. Verified end-to-end with real `.cto` programs via `certo.exe`: `Float32` arithmetic (`+`) works with zero special-casing (confirmed `BinOp::Add` is generic — just `unify(lt, rt)` — over any self-unifying type); full round-trip `intToFloat32 → float32ToFloat → floatToFloat32 → float32ToText` prints correctly, including the fractional-precision case that caught the HIR bug; `Text.charAt` correctly bounds-checks (`None` past the end); `Char.fromInt(72)` → `'H'`, `isAlpha`/`isDigit`/`toLowerCase` all verified against real ASCII values including a real digit-character extracted from a string (`'4'` → `Char.toInt` → `52`). 6 new `crates/typeck` tests (param/return type-checking, arithmetic, `Float32`/`Char` exclusivity from `Float`/`Text`/`Int`), 8 new `crates/stdlib` tests (signature registration + C-runtime-symbol presence). Full workspace test suite clean. | `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir`, `crates/codegen`, `crates/llvm`, `crates/stdlib`, `crates/cli` |
| 82 | `statemachine` `on_enter`/`invariant` codegen — previously parsed and silently dropped | Investigating found **two separate, inconsistent statemachine codegen paths** — only one (`crates/codegen/src/emit_statemachine.rs`'s desugar-to-Certo-source, spliced in before the real pipeline runs) is reachable from `certo build`; the other (`emit_module.rs`'s struct-based emitter) is dead code for `build`, only reached via `certo bench`, and was left untouched. The live path's machine value was a *bare enum* — transition params were accepted for signature compatibility but never stored anywhere, so there was nowhere for `on_enter`/`invariant` bodies to reference machine data at all (the spec's own example, `invariant Active: self.paymentMethod.isSome()`, needs a `self` to read). Redesigned it to desugar into a real struct instead: a `{Name}State` enum plus `{Name} = { state: {Name}State, f1: T1?, f2: T2?, … }`, `f1`/`f2`/… being the `Option`-wrapped union of every transition's event params (`None` until set). `on_enter`/`invariant` bodies are spliced into the generated source as genuine Certo expressions via `certo_fmt::fmt_expr` pretty-printing the already-parsed AST back to text — not a separate, necessarily-partial AST/HIR/MIR emission path — with `self` bound to the machine's new value (state already updated) in scope. User decisions (semantics undefined by spec): `on_enter` runs after the state commits; a violated `invariant` panics (checked once, right after `on_enter`, using the same panic-on-violation precedent as item 81 and the pre-existing invalid-transition check). **Found and fixed an unrelated, more severe, pre-existing bug while wiring the invariant panic**: `panic(msg)`'s Certo-level type is `∀a. Text -> a` (usable in any expression position), but `certo_panic` is genuinely `noreturn void` in C — unlike an ordinary `Unit`-returning Certo function (which fakes an assignable `int64_t` zero return), assigning a `void` call's result is a hard C compile error. This broke `panic(...)` used as an `if`/match branch *anywhere* in Certo, not just in generated state-machine code (confirmed with a minimal, unrelated repro) — nothing previously exercised this since no test called the bare `panic`/`unreachable`/`todo` builtins at all. Fixed in `crates/codegen/src/emit_mir.rs`'s `Rvalue::Call`/`Terminator::Call` emission: a call to `panic` specifically is now emitted as a bare statement, not an assignment (`unreachable`/`todo` deliberately left alone — they're zero-arg C macros with their own, separate, unrelated argument-count bug, not touched here). Verified end-to-end with real `.cto` programs via `certo.exe`: `on_enter` hooks fire (confirmed via `println` side effects) after the state visibly updates; a satisfied invariant lets a transition through silently; a violated one panics with the invariant's own source text in the message; a real data field (`Option<Int>`, read via `match self.amount { Some(a) => … None => … }`) is correctly readable from an invariant. Also confirmed, but explicitly out of scope and *not* fixed here: `certo_panic` doesn't flush stdout before terminating, so `println` output written just before a panic can be lost — reproduces with zero state-machine involvement, an unrelated, pre-existing bug, flagged separately. 6 new `crates/codegen` tests (2 updated for the new struct shape, 4 new: field accumulation, `on_enter` ordering, invariant panic text, bare `panic()`-in-branch). Full workspace test suite clean. | `crates/codegen` |

| 64 | Multiple integer types (`Int8`, `Int16`, `Int32`, `UInt`) — added to `Ty` enum, unification, display, typeck name lookup, C codegen (`int8_t`/`int16_t`/`int32_t`/`uint64_t`), LLVM backend, and REPL auto-print | `crates/typeck`, `crates/codegen`, `crates/llvm`, `crates/cli` |
| 100 | Row polymorphism — `fn getName<R: { name: Text }>(record: R): Text = record.name`. AST gained a `Bound` enum (`Trait`/`Row`) so a type param can carry a record-shape bound alongside/instead of a trait bound; parser accepts `{ field: Ty, ... }` in the bound position. Bound satisfaction is checked as a post-hoc pass at call sites (direct calls and pipe calls both go through `resolve_callee`/`apply_row_check` in `infer_expr.rs`), mirroring the existing trait-bound-checking architecture rather than baking row constraints into core unification — the row-bounded param is instantiated and unified as an ordinary fresh type variable, then afterward its resolved type is checked against the required fields (present + unifiable) with a new E0212 diagnostic for a missing field. Works for both exact-shape and superset-shape record arguments, and for `Type.method(...)` dot-call syntax as well as bare-name calls and pipes (see item 101). | `crates/ast`, `crates/parser`, `crates/fmt`, `crates/traits`, `crates/typeck`, `crates/cli` |
| 103 | Bug fixed — `??` (null-coalesce) was left-associative, breaking natural fallback chains | Found while writing a `pg_version.cto` exercise: `getEnv("DATABASE_URL") ?? arg(1) ?? default` failed to typecheck because `a ?? b ?? c` parsed as `(a ?? b) ?? c`, and typeck requires `??`'s right operand to be non-optional — but `b` (`arg(1)`) is itself `Text?`. Independently confirmed as a real, previously-hit trap: `examples/runsql.cto` already had a manual-parens workaround for the identical pattern (`arg(2) ?? (getEnv("PG_CONNSTR") ?? "")`). Every other language with a null-coalescing operator (C# `??`, Kotlin `?:`, Swift `??`) makes it right-associative for exactly this reason. Fixed `parse_coalesce` in `crates/parser/src/parse_expr.rs` to recurse on the right instead of looping, mirroring the existing right-associative `**` (`parse_power`) pattern — `a ?? b ?? c` now parses as `a ?? (b ?? c)`. 2 new parser tests (chain is right-associative; single `??` still parses) and 1 new typeck test (a 3-level chain with an optional middle value type-checks). | `crates/parser` |
| 95 | Wired `certo-effects` into `certo build`/`certo check` | `run_typeck` in `crates/cli/src/main.rs` now calls `certo_effects::check_module` right after the trait-bound check, converting `EffectError`s into E0400-E0404 diagnostics. Verified against every example `.cto` (no false positives) and against hand-written violations of each rule (undeclared effect, `await` outside `[async]`, `unsafe {}` outside `[unsafe]`) — all now correctly rejected. **Real limitation surfaced by turning this on:** `certo_effects::build_env` only scans the *current module's own* `fn`/`impl`/`trait` declarations (`crates/effects/src/effect_env.rs`) — it has no equivalent of `certo_stdlib::seed_stdlib`, so calls to stdlib functions (`println`, `dbConnect`, `dbExec`, etc.) contribute **no inferred effect at all**. A `[pure]` function that calls `println`/`dbExec` directly is silently accepted — the checker only catches effect violations that flow through calls to other functions declared in the same module, or through `await`/`unsafe {}`/`db.transaction {}` syntax. This is the same class of gap as row polymorphism's `Type.method(...)` limitation: real, but scoped out of "wire it in." See item 105. | `crates/cli`, `crates/effects` |
| 102 | Wired trait-bound checking at generic call sites (`check_call_bounds`) into `certo build`/`certo check` | `check_call_bounds` (item was previously dead code) is now driven by a new best-effort static walker, `check_generic_call_bounds` in `crates/traits/src/bounds.rs`, wired into `certo_traits::check_module` (already invoked by the CLI, so no CLI changes were needed for this half). For every call `f(args...)` to a module-local function/impl-method with a `Trait`-bounded type param `T`, where the argument at `T`'s position is a record literal `TypeName { .. }`, verifies `TypeName` satisfies the bound — same "best-effort, syntactic, no inference" philosophy as the pre-existing `check_dbquery_typed_bounds`, whose recursive AST walker was refactored into a shared, reusable `for_each_call`/`for_each_body` pair (both checks now use it, replacing two near-duplicate ~130-line walkers with one). 4 new tests: satisfying/unsatisfying record-literal arguments, and confirmation that a non-literal (variable) argument is correctly *not* flagged (no false positives). Dot-call routing for impl methods (item 101) landed in the same session — see that item for the shared fix and the impl-method name-collision bug it also caught here. | `crates/traits` |
| 106 | Bug fixed — two `certo-effects` diagnostic-quality bugs, invisible until item 95 wired the checker into the CLI | (1) `EffectErrorKind::ImpureCallInPure`'s `callee` field was always constructed as `String::new()`, so the diagnostic read `pure function `f` calls ``, which requires ...` with empty backticks. (2) The same violation fired **two** diagnostics for one root cause — a generic per-effect branch and a separate "pure function calling any effectful callee" sweep both triggered, emitting E0400 and E0401 for the same span. Root cause of both: `InferredEffects::origins` (`crates/effects/src/infer_effects.rs`) never tracked *which named function* introduced an inherited effect, and `check_fn_body` (`crates/effects/src/check_effects.rs`) ran two independent, overlapping passes over the same origins. Fixed by adding `callee: Option<String>` to each origin (`Some(name)` for a call whose declared effects were inherited, `None` for a direct syntactic form like `await`/`unsafe {}`/`db.transaction {}`), and consolidating to a single pass: a pure function's call to a named effectful callee now gets exactly one `ImpureCallInPure` with the real callee name; a direct syntactic effect gets exactly one of the existing specific messages (`UnsafeOutsideUnsafe`/`TransactionOutsideDbWrite`/etc.), never both. Also fixed a latent third bug found in the same pass: the old generic branch had no dedicated `Fallible` arm, so `?` inside a `[pure]` function was incorrectly flagged even though the crate's own design intent (stated in a comment) was to allow it — now explicitly skipped alongside `Pure`. 1 new test asserting the real callee name and exactly one error; 1 existing test's assertion narrowed from "either generic kind" to the specific, now-deduplicated `UnsafeOutsideUnsafe`. | `crates/effects` |
| 105 | Wired stdlib effect-seeding into `certo_effects` — `[pure]` functions calling stdlib I/O are now checked | New `certo_stdlib::seed_stdlib_effects` (`crates/stdlib/src/effects_seed.rs`) hand-registers `Effect::Io`/`Effect::Fallible` for every genuinely effectful stdlib function (`println`, `dbConnect`, `dbExec`, `readFile`, `Http.get`, etc.), and `certo_effects` gained `build_env_seeded`/`check_module_seeded` (mirroring `certo_typeck::check_module_seeded`) so a pre-seeded environment can be merged with the module's own declarations. Wired into `run_typeck` in `crates/cli/src/main.rs`. `fn f(): Int [pure] = { println("x"); 1 }` now correctly produces E0401 naming `println` as the offending callee — the exact motivating case for item 95. **Hand-maintained, not derived from source:** originally attempted to parse `certo_stdlib::certo_sources()` (the `*_CERTO` doc-text constants) directly instead of hand-listing function names, since those already carry `[io]` annotations — but most of those constants aren't valid, parseable Certo (see item 107), so the names/effects here were extracted from that text by hand instead. Dot-qualified names (`Query.list`, `Http.get`, ...) are registered too, and — as of item 101's fix — are now actually reachable at `Type.method(...)` call sites, e.g. `fn f(): Int [pure] = { Http.get(url); 1 }` correctly rejects naming `Http.get`. 2 new tests (known I/O functions get an entry; known-pure functions don't). | `crates/effects`, `crates/stdlib`, `crates/cli` |
| 101 | Fixed the shared `Type.method(...)` dot-call blind spot across row polymorphism (100), generic call-bound checking (102), and effects checking (105) | All three checkers walked the AST looking for a call's callee as a bare `Expr::Path`; `Type.method(...)`/`x.method(...)` parses as `Expr::App{ func: Expr::Field{ expr, field }, .. }` instead, so none of them ever fired for dot-call syntax — despite each one's underlying check being fully correct once it actually saw the call. Fixed with the same pattern in all three crates: recognize `Expr::Field{ expr: Path([Name]), field }` where `Name` starts uppercase (the exact heuristic `certo_typeck`'s own `Expr::Field` type-inference arm already used to resolve a qualified call's *type* — just never reused for the bound/effect checks riding alongside it) and resolve it to a `"Name.field"` qualified lookup key. `crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs`'s `resolve_callee` gained this (extracted into a shared `qualified_call_name` helper, also now used by the pre-existing `Expr::Field` infer arm instead of duplicating the heuristic); `crates/traits/src/bounds.rs` and `crates/effects/src/infer_effects.rs` each gained an equivalent `callee_lookup_key`. **Found and fixed a real latent bug along the way:** `certo-traits`' `collect_generic_fns` and `certo-effects`' `collect_fn_effects` both keyed impl methods by *bare* method name — two different `impl` blocks declaring a same-named generic/effectful method would silently collide in the lookup `HashMap`, with whichever was declared last winning. Both now key impl methods `"Type.method"`, matching how `certo_typeck` already keyed `row_bounds` for impl methods (which is what avoided this exact bug there). Verified end-to-end: `Caller.getName(NoName { id: 1 })` now correctly raises E0212 (row bound), a dot-called generic trait-bounded method now raises E0305, and `Http.get(...)` inside a `[pure]` function now correctly raises E0401 naming `Http.get`. 6 new tests across the three crates (dot-call now checked in each; a lowercase-receiver false-positive guard; two same-named-method-on-different-impls non-collision tests). | `crates/typeck`, `crates/traits`, `crates/effects` |
| 129 | Fixed `intToFloat`/`floatToInt` `Float`-truncation (flagged separately by item 75) and replaced HIR's two hand-maintained stdlib-return-type tables with one mechanically derived from `certo_stdlib::seed_stdlib` | Root cause was exactly what item 75 flagged: `crates/hir/src/lower.rs` runs its own separate, typeck-independent type inference for calls, and its `stdlib_ret_type`/`qualified_stdlib_ret_type` lookup tables never had `intToFloat`/`floatToInt` added — so `Expr::App`'s `call_ty` fell through to `Ty::Error`, which `ty_to_c.rs` maps to `int64_t`, and MIR's call-lowering (`mir/src/lower.rs`'s `_call` temp, `declare_local("_call", ..., expr.ty.clone())`) uses that HIR type directly for the result's C-declared type — so `val f = intToFloat(3)` declared `f` as `int64_t`, and C's implicit `double`→`int64_t` conversion silently truncated it before any further arithmetic ran (confirmed: `val g = f / 2.0; println(floatToText(g))` printed `1`, not `1.5`). Rather than hand-patch just these two names and leave the next missing function as a standing landmine (`seed.rs` has ~150 concrete-returning functions total — Text/DateTime/Decimal/Json/Http/etc — any one of which had the exact same latent gap), replaced both hand-maintained tables with a single `stdlib_ret_types()` (`OnceLock`-cached `HashMap<String, Ty>`) built by actually running `certo_stdlib::seed_stdlib` into a fresh `TypeEnv` — the same `TypeEnv` typeck itself checks every call against — and keeping every entry whose registered type is a concrete `Ty::Fn` (not `Forall`-wrapped, no free `Ty::Var` in its `ret`), keyed by whatever name `seed.rs` registered it under (bare top-level name or qualified `Type.method`, covering both of the old tables' shapes in one pass). Generic (`Forall`) producers like `List.get<T>` are still skipped here, unchanged — their result still comes from `generic_container_ret`'s per-callsite structural recovery below it in the same fallback chain. Required adding a `certo-hir` → `certo-stdlib` dependency (confirmed no cycle: `certo-stdlib` only depends on `certo-ast`/`certo-typeck`/`certo-effects`, none of which depend on `certo-hir`). Verified: 3 new `crates/hir` tests (`intToFloat`/`floatToInt` call type is `Float`/`Int` not `Ty::Error`; a qualified-name (`Char.toInt`) spot-check confirming the mechanical table covers both naming shapes without a hand-added entry). Full workspace build and test suite clean (0 failures across every crate). Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`: the exact item-75 repro (`val f = intToFloat(3); val g = f / 2.0; println(floatToText(g))`) now correctly prints `1.5`; `floatToInt(9.9)` correctly truncates to `9`.
