Added `### E0508`–`### E0526` (`crates/dbschema/src/error.rs` — table/column literal validation for `Query`/`Mutation` builders, join/alias handling, mutation-kind restrictions, live schema-sync drift) and `### E0600`–`### E0601` (`crates/cli/src/main.rs`'s `hir_error_to_diagnostic` — unresolved name at HIR lowering, unsupported generic construct) sections, matching the file's own established cause/fix/example format exactly; extended the TOC with new `E0508–E0526 DB query DSL` and `E0600–E0601 HIR lowering` ranges. **Caught and fixed a real accuracy bug in my own first draft before it shipped**: the initial write-up for all 21 entries used the *internal* `DbError::message()`/`LowerErrorKind` format strings (`crates/dbschema/src/error.rs`) as the source of truth — but a real end-to-end `certo check` run showed the actual printed message differs from that internal string for most of these (e.g. E0508's internal message reads `` `Query.from("Ghost")` — no `type Ghost` declared in this module ``, but the real CLI output, from a separate `db_error_to_diagnostic` conversion function in `crates/cli/src/main.rs` that rewords and restructures message/label/note independently, reads `` `Query.from("Ghost")` — no such table `` with a separate `note:` line). Re-derived and rewrote every one of the 21 entries against `db_error_to_diagnostic`'s real `Diagnostic::error(...)`/`.with_label(...)`/`.with_note(...)` calls instead, so the documented reproduction now matches character-for-character what `certo check` actually prints — the same class of internal-vs-user-facing mismatch this project has hit before (item 159/165's own diagnostic-wiring work), caught here by verifying against the real compiled binary rather than trusting the first plausible-looking source read. 2 new unit tests in `crates/cli/src/main.rs` (`explain_code_finds_e0210_through_e0215` from item 160, and a new `explain_code_finds_e0508_through_e0526_and_e0600_e0601` covering all 21). Full workspace test suite clean, no regressions. Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`: a genuine `Query.from("Ghost")` unknown-table repro now fails with `E0508` and `--explain` prints the corrected write-up, confirmed to match the real printed error text exactly, not just similarly. | `docs/ERROR-REFERENCE.md`, `crates/cli` |

| 156 | `certo db pull --url <dsn>` now works — an explicit connection string overrides `DATABASE_URL`/`.env` | `cmd_db_pull` (`crates/cli/src/main.rs`) previously only accepted `-o/--out` and `--schema` — `--url` hit the CLI's own "unknown option" error path. No other `certo db` subcommand had an existing `--url`/`--dsn` precedent to match (checked first, per the item's own note, before inventing anything) — `resolve_database_url_for_sync` (used by `certo db migrate`/schema-sync) only reads `DATABASE_URL`/`.env`, no flag override anywhere in the file. Added `--url` with the standard precedence order: explicit flag first, then `DATABASE_URL`, then `.env` — the same shape `--release`'s existing `[targets.production].schema` override already establishes elsewhere in this file for the identical env var. Updated `--help` text and `docs/CLI-TOOLCHAIN.md`'s usage examples. Not unit-tested — `cmd_db_pull` is an imperative, I/O-performing function (`psql` subprocess calls, `process::exit`) with no pure argument-parsing piece to isolate, matching how CLI flag-handling code elsewhere in this file is verified (real invocation, not a synthetic harness) — extracting one just for this flag would be a bigger refactor than the fix itself. Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe` against a real local PostgreSQL server: with `DATABASE_URL` deliberately set to an unreachable bogus host, `certo db pull --url "host=localhost dbname=postgres user=postgres"` correctly used the `--url` value instead and successfully introspected the real schema (proving real precedence, not just accepted-and-ignored); with neither `--url` nor `DATABASE_URL`/`.env` set, the original clear error path still fires, now also mentioning `--url` as an option. | `crates/cli`, `docs/CLI-TOOLCHAIN.md` |

| 158 | `certo fmt --diff` now works — prints a real unified diff of what would change, without writing | No existing diffing approach anywhere in the toolchain to reuse (checked `cmd_db_diff` first — schema-drift comparison, not text-line diffing) and no diff crate already a dependency; matching this project's pattern of small, self-contained implementations over pulling in a crate for one CLI flag, wrote a minimal LCS-based line diff in a new `crates/cli/src/diff.rs` module (mirrors the existing `cmd_doc.rs`/`cmd_repl.rs`-style per-command module split, rather than growing the already-3600-line `main.rs` further): classic LCS dynamic-programming table + backtrack for a minimal edit script, then grouped into unified-diff hunks with 3 lines of context, merging nearby changes into one hunk when their gap is within `2 × context` — real hunk headers (`@@ -a,b +c,d @@`) with correct line-count arithmetic via running old/new cursors computed once over the whole edit script, not per-hunk (so a hunk opened by an Insert or Delete, not just a Keep, still reports the right starting line number). **Design decision**: `--diff` implies dry-run (no writes) on its own, matching the common `--diff` convention from tools like `black --diff`, rather than requiring `--check --diff` together or writing-the-file-while-also-diffing-it (which would make the diff always look like a no-op immediately afterward, confusing) — `--check --diff` together still works identically, since `--diff` already implies `--check`'s own no-write/exit-1-on-changes behavior. 6 new unit tests in `crates/cli/src/diff.rs` (identical input produces no diff; a single-line change shows correct `-`/`+`/context lines; pure insertion/pure deletion each shown correctly with no spurious context-only changes; two distant changes stay as separate hunks; two nearby changes merge into one — pins the hunk-merging threshold itself, not just that hunks exist at all). Full workspace test suite clean, no regressions. Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe` on a genuinely misformatted file (`fn add(a: Int,b: Int): Int = a+b`): `certo fmt --diff` printed a correct unified diff (`-`/`+` on the two changed lines, ` ` context on the unchanged ones) and exited 1 without touching the file (confirmed byte-identical to the original afterward); `--check --diff` together produced byte-identical output to `--diff` alone; plain `certo fmt` (no flags) still really writes the reformatted file, confirmed unchanged from before this item. `docs/CLI-TOOLCHAIN.md` and `docs/GUIDE.md` updated with the new flag. | `crates/cli`, `docs/CLI-TOOLCHAIN.md`, `docs/GUIDE.md` |

| 154 | `certo repl --connect <dsn>` now works — the REPL can run real DB queries, via a fresh-per-call `conn(): Int` function | Investigated first, since the item's own note flagged this might be bigger than plumbing: confirmed the REPL had **zero real DB support at all**, not just an unwired flag — `try_compile_and_run` (`crates/cli/src/cmd_repl.rs`) hardcoded `certo_stdlib::full_c_runtime_with_db(false)`, so `dbConnect`/`dbQuery` would never link even with a DSN available. There's also a genuine design question the spec doesn't address: every REPL turn recompiles the whole session and runs a brand-new subprocess (no persistent interpreter state), so "connect" can't mean "open one socket and reuse it" the way `psql` does. Flagged to the user mid-investigation; chose the fuller design — auto-establish a connection every turn rather than just exposing the DSN as an env var. **Wired `--connect <dsn>`** through `main.rs`'s dispatch into `cmd_repl`'s own arg parsing (previously took zero arguments). **Fixed the DB-linking gap**: `full_c_runtime_with_db`/libpq linking now gated on whether `--connect` was passed, mirroring item 165b's identical fix for the test runner — gated (not unconditional) so an ordinary non-DB REPL session doesn't pay extra compile time linking libpq on every single turn. **Found and fixed a real, separate, pre-existing Windows-only bug found while verifying, unrelated to DB or `--connect` at all**: the REPL's own compile command never linked `-lshell32`/`-luser32` or set `/entry:wmainCRTStartup`, even though `certo_codegen::emit_module` unconditionally generates a `wmain`/`CommandLineToArgvW`-based entry point on Windows for every compiled module (`crates/codegen/src/emit_module.rs`) — `certo build`'s own invocation already links these; the REPL's separate compile command never did. Confirmed via a plain `println("hello")` REPL session failing with `undefined symbol: CommandLineToArgvW` — the REPL had likely never successfully compiled and run anything on Windows before this fix. **Design decision on how `conn` is exposed, changed twice during implementation, both times based on direct testing rather than assumption**: first attempt bound `val conn = dbConnect(...)` as a *module-level* declaration — direct testing showed `conn` was always a NULL/zero handle, traced to a real, separate, deeper codegen bug (see item 168, split out): a top-level `val` whose initializer is a function call silently compiles to a bare placeholder `static void* certo_conn = /* expr */0;`, never actually running the initializer. Second attempt moved `val conn = dbConnect(...)` inside `main()`'s body (a local, not module-level, `val`) — this avoided the codegen bug but only made `conn` visible to bare expressions typed at the prompt, not to session-level declarations (`fn foo() = { dbQuery(conn, ...) }` failed to resolve `conn`, confirmed directly) — a real gap for the REPL's own most natural "define a reusable query function" pattern. **Final design**: `conn` is a zero-argument top-level *function*, `fn conn(): Int [io] = dbConnect("...")`, injected before both session declarations and `main()`/the `?`-helper — a function isn't subject to the static-initializer bug at all (its body is a real function, called fresh each time, not evaluated once at load time), and being an ordinary top-level declaration it's visible from both a bare per-turn expression and a session-level `val`/`fn`. Callers write `conn()`, not bare `conn` — an honest reflection of the real "fresh connection every call" semantics, not just a workaround. 6 new unit tests in `crates/cli/src/cmd_repl.rs` (DSN escaping round-trips through the real lexer/parser — not just "looks escaped"; no DSN means no `conn()`/import; a DSN injects `fn conn()` ahead of both `main()` and the declaration-probe path; the `?`-using `_repl_try()` wrapper also gets `conn()` declared ahead of it). Full workspace test suite clean, no regressions (confirmed a non-DB REPL session — bare expressions, a session-defined function — still works exactly as before this item). Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe` against a real local PostgreSQL server: `conn()` returns a real, non-zero, working connection handle (confirmed via `dbError` returning empty, not "connection failed"); a bare `dbExec(conn(), "insert ...", [])` typed at the prompt correctly inserted and persisted a real row (confirmed via a direct `psql` query afterward, and via the REPL's own auto-printed row-count); a session-level `fn countRows(): Int [io] = List.len(dbQuery(conn(), ...))` defined in one turn and called in a later turn correctly returned the real, live row count. Not attempted: fixing the deeper top-level-val-with-call-initializer codegen bug itself (split out as item 168 — a separate, larger, unrelated fix affecting the whole language, not just the REPL). | `crates/cli` |

| 168 | Fixed a real, general, silent-wrong-answer bug: a module-level `val name = <expr>` whose initializer isn't a compile-time-constant literal now actually runs its initializer, instead of always evaluating to a placeholder zero/NULL | Confirmed the real scope first: `crates/codegen/src/emit_module.rs`'s global-declaration emission (`const_expr_to_c`) only ever produced a real C expression for a literal `Int`/`Float`/`Bool`/`Str`/`Unit` — anything else (a function call, a `++` string concat, any non-literal expression) fell through to a bare placeholder, `static T name = /* expr */0;`, silently discarding the real initializer. Confirmed general (not REPL/DB-specific, despite being found while building item 154): reproduced with a plain hand-written `.cto` file, no REPL involved. **Fix**: reuses the existing, already-correct function-lowering pipeline instead of trying to render an arbitrary expression as a single C expression (the abandoned `simple_expr_to_c`/`const_expr_to_c` fallbacks already in the file had tried and given up on exactly this). Each non-literal `val`'s initializer becomes the body of a synthesized zero-arg `__certo_init_<name>` function, lowered and emitted through the exact same `lower_fn`/`emit_fn_with_prefix` path an ordinary function uses — no new expression-lowering logic to get right, since function bodies (including a bare-call body, `fn conn(): Int = dbConnect(...)`) were already proven correct by item 154. The global itself is declared uninitialized (`static T name;`, zero-init by plain C semantics — same runtime value as before this fix if the assignment is never reached, so no new failure mode) and assigned from `name = __certo_init_name();` in the generated bootstrap, right after `certo_main_init(...)` and before the user's own `main()` body runs. Literal initializers are completely untouched — still real, valid C constant expressions, zero behavior change, zero risk for the common case. **Found and fixed a real ordering bug in this fix's own first draft, caught via direct testing before it shipped**: emitting every init function's *body* in one early batch (mirroring how lifted lambdas are already handled) breaks the moment one init body references *another* module-level global — `val twenty = double(ten)` failed with a genuine C `undeclared identifier 'certo_ten'` error, since `__certo_init_twenty`'s body was emitted before `certo_ten`'s own declaration existed anywhere in the file. Fixed by emitting each init function's body at the *same point* as its own const's declaration (in the existing interleaved Const/Fn emission loop), not in an early batch — matching the exact same source-order-dependent rule ordinary top-level Certo declarations already have (forward declarations exist for functions; there was never one for globals, so a global's first appearance in the file *is* its declaration, same as before this fix). 6 new unit tests in `crates/codegen/src/tests.rs` (a literal initializer is untouched — no init function generated at all; a call-expression initializer gets a real init function, not the placeholder `/* expr */0`; the init call happens *before* `certo_main()` in the bootstrap, checked by textual position, not just presence; a const referencing an earlier non-literal const compiles and orders correctly — pins the ordering bug fix specifically; a non-Call non-literal expression, `++` string concat, also goes through the same real-init path). Full workspace test suite clean, no regressions. Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`: the exact `fn five(): Int = 5` / `val x = five()` repro from item 154's own investigation now prints `5`, not `0`, both via plain `certo run` and via the REPL's session mechanism; a program with four top-level vals (a literal, a call, a string concat, and a call referencing an earlier call-initialized val) all printed correct values in one run; re-ran item 154's own dropped scenario — `val rows = getRows()` where `getRows()` runs a real `dbQuery` — against a live PostgreSQL server, which now correctly prints the real row count instead of the `0` it printed when item 154 shipped (that gap is what led to discovering this bug in the first place). Not attempted: revisiting item 154's own `fn conn()` design now that a plain `val conn = dbConnect(...)` would also work — item 154's design already ships and works correctly, and there was no reason to touch already-verified, working code as a side effect of this item. | `crates/codegen` |

| 155 | `certo doc --serve --port <n>` now works — fixed the spec's command-name typo (`certo docs` → `certo doc`) and built the real live-serve capability, not just the name | Investigated the BACKLOG's own scoping note first: it suggested reusing item 130's `certo_http_serve` — checked, and that turned out to be C runtime code embedded in *compiled Certo programs* (`Http.serve(port, handler)`, `crates/stdlib/src/http.rs`), not a Rust utility the CLI binary itself can call — a real, separate implementation was needed, not a reuse. Flagged this to the user before proceeding; chose to build the real capability rather than just the name fix. Wrote a minimal, self-contained, single-threaded static-file HTTP/1.1 server directly in Rust (new `crates/cli/src/static_serve.rs`, no new dependency — same hand-rolled-over-crate approach as item 158's diff module): GET-only, percent-decodes the request path, strips query strings, maps `/` and any trailing-slash path to `index.html`, guesses `Content-Type` from the file extension, and — the one property a "serve a folder" server must not get wrong even for local dev use — rejects any path containing a `..` segment rather than trying to canonicalize-and-compare (which would require the target file to already exist). Wired `--serve`/`--port <n>` (default 4000, matching the spec's own example) into `cmd_doc`; after generating the docs, `--serve` starts the server over the output directory and blocks until killed. Fixed the command-name typo in both `docs/Certo_Language_Specification.md` and `docs/CERTO-SPEC.md` (a near-duplicate legacy copy also cross-referenced by other docs, found to have the identical stale name — fixed both for consistency rather than leaving one accurate and one not) and documented the new flags in `docs/CLI-TOOLCHAIN.md`. 8 new unit tests in `crates/cli/src/static_serve.rs` (request-line parsing, query-string stripping, percent-decoding, malformed-request rejection, root/trailing-slash → `index.html`, ordinary path mapping, `..` traversal rejected — including a percent-encoded variant, extension-based content-type guessing). Full workspace test suite clean, no regressions. **Verification note, disclosed for full transparency**: while testing `--serve` end-to-end, ran `certo doc` against a scratch file sitting in the repo's own root directory — its default output directory (`docs/` next to the source file) collided with the repository's own real `docs/` folder, and a subsequent cleanup `rm -rf docs` deleted the real directory (all spec/guide/reference docs) rather than just the scratch output. Caught immediately via `git status`, fully and cleanly restored via `git checkout -- docs/` (confirmed byte-identical to `HEAD` afterward, zero data loss) — the deletion was never committed. Redid the verification using the session's own scratchpad directory instead of the repo root. Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`, this time from the scratchpad: `certo doc check.cto --serve --port 4712` generated real docs and served them — a real `GET /` returned `200` with the actual generated content (confirmed the function name `add` appears in the response body, not a stub); a missing path correctly returned `404`; a raw-socket request (bypassing any client-side URL normalization) for `GET /../secret.txt` correctly returned `404`, confirming the traversal protection holds even against a request a browser would never send but a hand-crafted one could. | `crates/cli`, `docs/Certo_Language_Specification.md`, `docs/CERTO-SPEC.md`, `docs/CLI-TOOLCHAIN.md` |

| 157 | `certo new --template fullstack` now works — a real `@ui.generate` CRUD server scaffold, not just a spec/CLI template-list correction | Investigated first: confirmed `view`/`form`/`@ui.generate` (item 87) are understood *only* by the standalone `certo-ui` binary (`crates/ui`), never by the main compiler pipeline (exhaustive grep across `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir`, `crates/codegen` — zero matches) — so a naive "put `@ui.generate` directly in `src/main.cto`" template would not be `certo run`-able, breaking every other template's single-step convention. Found that `crates/ui/src/server.rs` exposes `pub fn emit_server(module: &Module) -> Result<String, UiError>` — the same function `certo-ui`'s own `main.rs` calls to produce `server.cto` — and added `certo-ui` as a path dependency of `crates/cli` (both already share the minimal `certo-ast`/`certo-parser` deps, no circularity) so `cmd_new` can call it directly instead of hand-duplicating what `certo-ui` produces. Design: `src/ui.cto` (a `type Task = { id: Text, title: Text, done: Bool }` record plus `@ui.generate(Task) { title: "Tasks" list: { columns: [id, title, done] } }`) is the hand-editable source of truth; `src/main.cto` is `fullstack_ui_source`'s output run through `certo_parser::parse` + `certo_ui::emit_server` *at scaffold time*, written out already-lowered and immediately `certo run`-able — matching every other template's single-step convention, and guaranteed to never drift from what the standalone `certo-ui` binary would itself produce, since it's the literal same function. Also scaffolds `migrations/001_create_task.cto` (a real `createTable task { id: Text primaryKey, title: Text, done: Bool }` migration). `id` is `Text`, not `Int` — found during end-to-end verification that an integer primary key with no DB-side default breaks the generated Create form's INSERT (the migrate DSL has no auto-increment/identity column support, and `id` is excluded from the Create form whenever it isn't listed in `list.columns`, so nothing would ever supply a value for it); switched to a user-supplied `Text` id, matching the one other real, shipped `@ui.generate`-adjacent example in the repo (`examples/fireworks_ui.cto`'s `custNo`/`productCde` Text primary keys). **Found and fixed a separate, independent, pre-existing bug while verifying the migration story**: `certo db create`/`certo migrate create`'s own scaffolded migration template (`crates/cli/src/main.rs`, the `"create"` arm of `cmd_migrate`) never had a `module X` header — `certo_parser::parse` requires one unconditionally, so *every* migration file that command ever generated was silently unparseable the moment `certo db migrate`/`certo db status` tried to load it (`error: expected Module, found Migration`). Fixed by prepending `module Migration\n\n` to that template too, not just my own; added a regression test (`migrate_create_template_has_a_module_header`). Also fixed the original complaint directly: `docs/CERTO-SPEC.md` and `docs/Certo_Language_Specification.md` both said `--template api│fullstack│lib` (missing `cli`, and `fullstack` didn't exist) — corrected to `default│api│lib│cli│fullstack` in both; `docs/GUIDE.md` separately claimed "Available templates: `cli` (default), `api`, `lib`" (wrong on multiple counts — `default` is the actual default, `fullstack` was missing) — corrected; added a `fullstack` row and full section to `docs/CLI-TOOLCHAIN.md`'s template table. 5 new unit tests in `crates/cli/src/main.rs` (`src/ui.cto`'s generated source parses and declares `Task` + `@ui.generate`; `emit_server` on it produces the expected `/task-list`/`/create-task` routes and a real `INSERT INTO task`; the generated `src/main.cto` output is itself valid, re-parseable Certo source; a regression guard pinning `id` as `Text` with `id` present in `list.columns`; the `migrate create` module-header regression guard). Full workspace test suite clean (`cargo test --workspace`), no regressions. Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`, from the session's scratchpad directory: `certo new myapp_full --template fullstack` → `certo migrate up` (real `CREATE TABLE task (...)` executed against a live local PostgreSQL 18) → `certo build src/main.cto` → ran the compiled server → `POST /create-task` with real form data → `GET /task-list` rendered the inserted row in its HTML table → confirmed the row via a direct `psql SELECT` against the live database → dropped the table and the temp project afterward. Not attempted: an "edit" flow smoke test beyond confirming `EditTask`'s form is generated (create+list, the harder end-to-end path involving a real DB write, was the priority); fixing the pre-existing `db/migrations/` (scaffolded by *every* template) vs. `migrations/` (what `certo db migrate` actually reads) directory-name mismatch for the other templates — flagged as a new Pending item below rather than silently touched here, since it's unrelated to `fullstack` specifically. | `crates/cli`, `crates/cli/Cargo.toml`, `docs/CERTO-SPEC.md`, `docs/Certo_Language_Specification.md`, `docs/GUIDE.md`, `docs/CLI-TOOLCHAIN.md` |

| 169 | `certo new` scaffolded a dead `db/migrations/` directory for every template, while `certo db migrate`/`certo db status`/`certo migrate create` actually read/wrote `<project_root>/migrations/` — fixed by wiring up `certo.toml`'s existing `[database] migrations` field instead of just picking one hardcoded path | Investigated the two options item 169's own note described (point `cmd_new` at `migrations/`, or change the reader's default to `db/migrations/`) and found a better third option first: `crates/cli/src/certo_toml.rs`'s `DatabaseSection.migrations: Option<String>` was already real, parsed TOML — explicitly documented in the module's own doc comment as "reserved for future toolchain work," matching the spec's own §11.4 example (`migrations = "db/migrations/"`, also `certo_toml.rs`'s own test fixture) — but nothing downstream had ever read it. Flagged this to the user before proceeding rather than silently picking a direction; chose to wire the existing field up properly instead of adding a second hardcoded path. New `migrations_dir(project_root: &Path) -> PathBuf` (`crates/cli/src/main.rs`) reads `certo.toml`'s `[database] migrations` if set, else defaults to `migrations/` — the exact path `load_migrations`/`cmd_migrate "create"` already hardcoded, so any existing project with no `[database]` section sees zero behavior change. Both `load_migrations` and `cmd_migrate`'s `"create"` arm now call it instead of hardcoding `project_root.join("migrations")`. `cmd_new` now writes `[database]\nmigrations = "db/migrations/"` into every template's scaffolded `certo.toml`, making the `db/migrations/` directory every template already created real for the first time; item 157's fullstack template's own special-cased `migrations/` directory (added as a stopgap while this item was still Pending) was removed and its seed migration now lives at the real `db/migrations/001_create_task.cto`. Updated `certo_toml.rs`'s module doc comment (`migrations` is no longer purely reserved) and `docs/CLI-TOOLCHAIN.md` (the `certo db create`/`certo migrate` sections now describe the configurable path and default; the `[database]` fields reference table row split so `migrations` shows as read, `schema`/`seeds` remain marked reserved; item 157's own `fullstack` template section corrected to `db/migrations/`). 3 new unit tests in `crates/cli/src/main.rs` (`migrations_dir` defaults to `migrations/` with no `certo.toml`; defaults the same way with a `certo.toml` present but no `[database]` section; honors an explicit `[database] migrations = "db/migrations/"`). Full workspace test suite clean, no regressions. Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe` from the session's scratchpad: `certo new` (default template) → `certo migrate create add_widgets` correctly wrote into `db/migrations/` (not `migrations/`, confirmed by checking both paths) → `certo migrate status` correctly found and listed it as pending, reading from the same configured path; re-ran item 157's fullstack scenario end-to-end from scratch (`certo new --template fullstack` → `certo migrate up`, a real `CREATE TABLE task` against live local PostgreSQL, migration file now read from `db/migrations/` → `certo build` → ran the compiled server → `POST /create-task` → `GET /task-list` rendered the real inserted row) to confirm the directory move didn't regress the working feature it originally motivated this item. | `crates/cli` |

| 164b | `Timestamp` now has real constructors of its own (`.now`/`.of`/`.parse`/`.inTimezone`/`.formatTz`), plus `Date.of` — additive, no rename of the already-shipped `DateTime` API | Investigated first: confirmed by direct testing that `DateTime` and `Timestamp` are genuinely distinct nominal types (a `DateTime.now()` value fails to type-check where a `Timestamp` param is expected, `E0200`), and that there was previously no way to construct a real `Timestamp` value at all. Presented the two real directions to the user before implementing — rename `DateTime`→`Timestamp` everywhere (spec's exact single name, but touches all 38 registered `DateTime.*` stdlib functions plus 3 example/doc files and 5 test files) vs. give `Timestamp` its own constructors additively (zero risk to the shipped `DateTime` API) — user chose the additive option. **Found a related, real, currently-dormant bug while investigating**: `needs_heap_box()` (`crates/typeck/src/ty.rs`) already special-cases `DateTime`/`Date`/`Duration`/etc. as pointer-sized opaque handles (matching `ty_to_c.rs`'s `int64_t` representation) but had never added `Timestamp` to that same list — unreachable before this item (nothing could construct a `Timestamp` value to expose it), but would have silently corrupted a `List<Timestamp>`/`Tuple`/`Option<Timestamp>` the moment real constructors existed. Fixed as a prerequisite of shipping this item, not a separate one. **Implementation**: `crates/stdlib/src/datetime.rs` gained two genuinely new C functions — `certo_timestamp_of(year, month, day, hour, minute, second, tz)` (converts the given wall-clock-in-`tz` components to a real UTC epoch via the same single-lookup timezone-offset approximation `certo_date_today_in` already uses) and `certo_date_of(year, month, day)` (UTC-midnight calendar date, matching `Date.today()`'s convention) — plus a `#define` bridge (`certo_timestamp_now`/`.parse`/`.inTimezone`/`.formatTz` → the existing, already-working `certo_datetime_*`/`certo_date_time_*` implementations), the exact same bridging pattern this file already used to reconcile codegen's mechanical `DateTime.*` → `certo_date_time_*` name-mangling with the real `certo_datetime_*` function names. `crates/stdlib/src/seed.rs` registers `Timestamp.now`/`Timestamp.of`/`Timestamp.parse`/`Timestamp.inTimezone`/`Timestamp.formatTz`/`Date.of` with real `Ty::Fn` signatures. Diverges from the spec's literal `Timestamp.parse(text): Result<Timestamp, ParseError>` in one respect, disclosed rather than silently matched: `ParseError` doesn't exist anywhere in this codebase (confirmed via grep — the same is true of `Text.toInt()`'s own spec-documented `Result<Int, ParseError>`, a separate, pre-existing, undocumented gap not touched here), so `Timestamp.parse` returns `Timestamp` directly, matching `DateTime.parseIso`'s real, already-shipped, non-`Result` behavior exactly rather than inventing new error-type machinery as a side effect of this item. 7 new unit tests: 2 in `crates/typeck/src/ty.rs` (`Timestamp.needs_heap_box()` matches `DateTime`'s — the regression guard for the dormant bug above; an ordinary user record type is unaffected), 5 in `crates/stdlib/src/tests.rs` (all six new functions are registered with the correct `Ty::Fn` signatures; `DATETIME_C` contains the two new real functions and all four bridge `#define`s, matching the file's exact text). Full workspace test suite clean, no regressions. Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`: a program constructing `Timestamp.now()`, `Timestamp.of(2026,3,15,13,30,0,tz)`, and `Timestamp.parse(...)`, then formatting all three via `Timestamp.inTimezone`/`Timestamp.formatTz`, plus `Date.of` — all produced correct, real values, not placeholders; separately verified `Timestamp.of`'s timezone-conversion math directly against a real DST-observing zone (`Europe/London`, July 2026 = BST/UTC+1): `Timestamp.of(2026, 7, 15, 13, 30, 0, london)` formatted back to UTC correctly showed `12:30:00`, confirming the offset subtraction is genuinely correct, not just plausible-looking; confirmed `.age` (item 164a) still works correctly on a real `Timestamp.now()`-constructed value; confirmed the `needs_heap_box` fix directly — pushed a `Timestamp` through `List.push`/`List.getOrPanic` and read back the exact correct value (would have corrupted before this fix, per the bug's own mechanism). Not attempted: renaming `DateTime`→`Timestamp` (the user's chosen direction doesn't need it); adding the rest of `DateTime`'s ~20-function surface (arithmetic, comparison, component accessors) under the `Timestamp` name — out of scope per the exact function list presented to and approved by the user, not silently expanded. | `crates/stdlib`, `crates/typeck` |

| 165 | `expect(x).toBe(y)`/`.toBeTrue()`/`.toBeFalse()`/`.toBeSome()`/`.toBeNone()`/`.toBeOk()`/`.toBeErr()` (spec §9.5) now work — a real, dedicated assertion form, not general dot-call (which doesn't exist in this language for arbitrary values) | Investigated first: confirmed dot-call on a value genuinely doesn't work anywhere in this language today, even for the simplest possible case (`Some(5).isSome()` fails `E0205`, same as item 162's own finding for `List`) — so `expect(x).toBe(y)` needed its own dedicated grammar, the same precedent `.age` already established, not a general method-dispatch mechanism. Investigating `.toBe`'s equality needs surfaced a second real question — `BinOp::Eq`'s codegen emits a raw C `(lhs == rhs)` for anything that isn't `Text`, a straight compile error for `Decimal`/record types (invalid on C structs) — the spec's own worked Money example would hit this immediately. Presented both findings and a scope choice to the user before implementing: ship `.toBe` scoped to whatever `==` already correctly supports (Int/Bool/Text/Float), leaving generic struct equality as its own future item, vs. also fixing `BinOp::Eq`'s codegen as part of this one. Chose the smaller, correctly-scoped option. **Design**: `expect<T>(x: T): T` is a real, honest identity function (`crates/stdlib/src/seed.rs`, C implementation is a `#define certo_expect certo_identity` bridge to the already-shipped `identity` — item 161) — it exists for spec-matching readability at the call site, not as a syntactic gate; the seven `.toBeXxx` matchers are recognized unconditionally by field name at the parser level (`crates/parser/src/parse_expr.rs`'s `parse_postfix`, exactly `.age`'s own precedent) regardless of what precedes them, so `x.toBe(y)` and `expect(x).toBe(y)` are equivalent — confirmed by a dedicated test. New `Expr::ExpectAssertion`/`ExpectMatcher` AST (`crates/ast/src/expr.rs`); typeck (`crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs`) reuses the exact same `unify` calls the equivalent hand-written code would (`.toBe` unifies actual/expected exactly like `BinOp::Eq`; `.toBeTrue`/`.toBeFalse` unify against `Bool`; `.toBeSome`/`.toBeNone` against a fresh `Option<_>`; `.toBeOk`/`.toBeErr` against a fresh `Result<_,_>`) — every failure mode is the pre-existing, already-documented `E0200`, no new error code needed. HIR lowering (`crates/hir/src/lower.rs`) desugars directly to the real `assert(cond, msg)` every hand-written test already calls — `.toBe(y)` → `assert(actual == y, "expected values to be equal")`, `.toBeFalse` → `assert(!actual, "expected false")`, and the four tag-checks call two pairs of small, genuinely new, independently-useful stdlib predicates added alongside this (`Option.isSome`/`Option.isNone` — thin `!= NULL`/`== NULL` wrappers matching this runtime's existing `None`-is-`NULL` convention; `Result.isOk`/`Result.isErr` — thin wrappers over the `__result_is_ok` primitive every `Result` combinator in `crates/stdlib/src/result.rs` already uses) rather than synthesizing a `match` inline. Verified the promised scope boundary held, not just assumed: `expect(a).toBe(b)` on two `Decimal` values fails to compile with the *same* pre-existing struct-argument limitation a plain `identity(a)` on a Decimal already has today (`identity`/`expect` are both `∀T. T -> T` passed through a `void*` slot) — confirmed by testing `identity(d"1.00")` in isolation and seeing the identical error shape, so this item introduced no new failure mode, exactly as scoped with the user. 27 new unit tests across every layer touched (7 parser: all matchers parse, `.toBe` requires exactly one arg, matcher works without a literal `expect(...)` receiver, an unrelated field named e.g. `.toString` is unaffected; 10 typeck: every matcher's happy path and its `E0200` failure mode, the whole expression's type is `Unit`; 6 HIR: the exact desugared shape for each matcher family; 4 stdlib: all five new registrations exist with correct signatures, both new C runtime files contain the expected symbols). Full workspace test suite clean, no regressions. Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe` and `certo-test.exe` (not just `certo check`): all seven matchers passing in one program; a deliberately-failing `expect(1+1).toBe(3)` panicked with `expected values to be equal` and a non-zero exit; a real `certo test` run against three `test { }` blocks (one passing, one deliberately failing, one exercising `.toBeSome`/`.toBeErr`) correctly reported `1 failed, 2 passed` — the actual spec-shaped `Stdlib.Test` workflow (`test "..." { expect(total).toBe(22) }`) working for real, not simulated. Split `asyncTest` out as item 165c (needs its own design decision, unrelated to this piece) — `dbTest` auto-rollback shipped earlier as item 165b (Completed). | `crates/ast`, `crates/parser`, `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir`, `crates/stdlib`, `crates/dbschema`, `crates/effects`, `crates/fmt`, `crates/resolve`, `crates/traits` |

| 162a | `List.flatMap`/`List.reduce` now work — the bounded half of item 162's missing aggregate functions | Investigated the full spec table first (§9.2) and found the six missing functions split into two genuinely different sizes of work — flagged to the user before implementing: `flatMap`/`reduce` are pure closure delegation, identical shape to the already-shipped `List.map`/`List.fold`; `sortBy`/`sumBy`/`minBy`/`maxBy` all need real per-call-site MIR synthesis (the same class of engineering as items 140-142's `compose`/`const`/`flip`) to compare/add a type only known concretely at each call site — split out as item 162b rather than rushed in here. `List.flatMap<A,B>(list: List<A>, f: A => List<B>): List<B>` is genuinely new: added to `BOXED_ABI_CALLEES` (`crates/mir/src/lower.rs`) alongside `List.map` — same single-element-typed-param-returning-something-else shape, needing the identical boxed-ABI lambda lift — and a real new C implementation (`certo_list_flat_map`, `crates/stdlib/src/collections.rs`) that maps each element to a sub-list via the callback then concatenates all the sub-lists in call order. `List.reduce<T,A>(list: List<T>, init: A, f: (A,T)=>A): A` is spec's own name for exactly `List.fold`'s already-shipped signature — same argument order (list, init, combiner) — so it reuses `fold`'s real implementation via a `#define certo_list_reduce certo_list_fold` bridge (the same reuse-via-macro-bridge pattern item 164b's `Timestamp.*` functions already established for `DateTime.*`) rather than duplicating working code. 7 new unit tests: 4 in `crates/stdlib/src/tests.rs` (both registered and polymorphic; `List.reduce`'s signature shape structurally matches `List.fold`'s, not just superficially; both new C symbols/the `#define` bridge are present in `COLLECTIONS_C`'s real text), 2 in `crates/codegen/src/tests.rs` (a lambda passed to `List.flatMap` gets the same boxed lift `List.map`'s own test already pins; `List.reduce` compiles to a real call naming `certo_list_reduce`, not a duplicate implementation). Full workspace test suite clean, no regressions. Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`: `List.flatMap([1,2,3], (x) => [x,x])` produced the correct 6-element `[1,1,2,2,3,3]`, including the empty-list edge case; `List.reduce` produced correct results for both a numeric sum and a `Text` concat (the latter throwing the identical, pre-existing, harmless pointer-qualifier compiler warning `List.fold` itself already produces for a `Text` accumulator — confirmed directly by compiling the equivalent `List.fold` call side by side, so `reduce` introduces no new warning class, only reuses the one `fold` already has). | `crates/stdlib`, `crates/mir` |
| 162b | `List.sortBy`/`minBy`/`maxBy`/`sumBy` now work, including for struct-element lists — the per-call-site-MIR-synthesis half of item 162 split out from `flatMap`/`reduce` (item 162a) | **Registration** (`crates/stdlib/src/seed.rs`): `List.sortBy<T,K>(List<T>, T=>K): List<T>`, `List.minBy`/`maxBy<T,K>(List<T>, T=>K): T?`, `List.sumBy<T,N>(List<T>, T=>N): N`, registered as unrestricted `Ty::Forall` matching the spec's own `K: Ord`/`N: Numeric` bounds — but the *key/numeric projection* `K`/`N` is deliberately restricted at typeck to `Int`/`Int8`/`Int16`/`Int32`/`UInt`/`Float`/`Float32` (new `E0710`, `TypeErrorKind::UnsupportedKeyType`, `crates/typeck/src/error.rs`+`infer_expr.rs`), a real scope decision confirmed by direct testing rather than assumed: this codebase's raw `<`/`>`/`+` C operators are only correct for those types — `Text`'s `<` is a raw pointer comparison, not lexicographic (`"apple" < "banana"` "works" today only by allocation-address coincidence — confirmed by inspecting the generated C, which has no `certo_text_eq`-style special case for `Lt`), and `Decimal` is a struct with no generic `<`/`+` to synthesize a call to. The list element type `T` itself is unrestricted, including structs. **MIR synthesis** (`crates/mir/src/lower.rs`): `lower_sort_by_call` builds a per-call-site comparator trampoline (boxed `void*` element params, matching `certo_list_sort`'s own fixed, fully-erased `CertoCmp` C ABI — confirmed `List.sort` is *not* in `BOXED_ABI_CALLEES`, so this needed its own boxing logic rather than reuse) that calls the key closure on both elements and compares via `BinOp::Lt`/`Gt`, then calls `List.sort`; `lower_min_max_by_call` reuses that comparator then calls `List.first`/`List.last`; `lower_sum_by_call` synthesizes a dedicated named summation loop (mirroring `HirExprKind::For`'s own hand-rolled loop lowering, including its `List.getOrPanic` Float/struct unboxing) rather than routing through `List.fold`'s own generic accumulator slot. **Three real, independently-diagnosed bugs found and fixed while getting this to actually compile and run**, none guessed — each confirmed via direct `--emit-c` inspection or a real `certo check`/`certo run` repro before being called a bug: (1) an unannotated inline lambda argument's own param type is never resolved without an external per-call-site hint (`crates/hir/src/lower.rs`'s established `needs_lambda_hint`/`lower_lambda_with_param_hint` mechanism) — `sortBy`/`minBy`/`maxBy`/`sumBy` were missing from both `needs_lambda_hint`'s allowlist *and* a separate, older, hand-maintained `stdlib_param_names()` table that gates whether the hint branch runs at all for a given callee — without both, the key lambda's own param compiled as a bare `int64_t` instead of a real `certo_fn_t`, failing with "called object type 'int64_t' is not a function"; (2) `generic_container_ret` (`crates/hir/src/lower.rs`) — the name-based table that back-fills a generic stdlib call's *return* type structurally, because HIR can't reliably reread typeck's own post-unification substitution for these calls — likewise never had `sortBy`/`minBy`/`maxBy`/`sumBy` added, so a `val sorted = List.sortBy(...)` binding's type stayed unresolved even after fix (1), silently miscompiling any later use of `sorted` on a struct-element list (`certo_list_get_or_panic(...)` used without the unboxing struct elements need — confirmed via `--emit-c` showing a raw, undereferenced `int64_t` used directly in `.field` access, a real C compile error); (3) the new E0710 typeck check itself never actually ran for *any* real call site: `List.sortBy(...)`'s callee parses as `Expr::Field` (`List` `.` `sortBy`), not `Expr::Path`, and the `Expr::App` arm's `fn_name` derivation only ever handled `Expr::Path` — so every module-qualified stdlib call's `fn_name` was silently `None` (confirmed by direct instrumentation), and E0710 constructed but never checked; fixed narrowly for just this check (re-deriving the name via the existing `qualified_call_name` helper `resolve_callee` already uses for the identical gap) rather than widening the shared `fn_name`/`arg_info` derivation all call sites use, which has separate, wider blast radius on labeled-arg reordering — flagged below as its own item rather than folded in. **A fourth bug was found and fixed as a direct consequence of fixing (3)**: once E0710 actually started running, it wrongly rejected every struct-element key that does field access (`List.sortBy(products, (p) => p.price)`, `Float` — a legitimate case) as "resolved to `T`", because typeck's own `Expr::Field` inference (`resolve_field_ty`) returns a brand-new, permanently disconnected fresh type variable for field access on a still-unbound base type, never `Float` — a real, structural limit of typeck's own resolution for this shape, not fixable by more unification. Fixed by having the typeck-level E0710 check stay silent on a bare, still-unresolved `Ty::Var` (deferring rather than guessing) and adding the equivalent, *correctly*-resolved check at HIR-lowering time instead (right after the per-call-site lambda hint from bug (1) is applied, when the key's real type — including through field access — is actually known); genuinely-unsupported struct-field-access keys are now caught there as `E0601` (`LowerErrorKind::Unsupported`) instead of `E0710`, documented as such in both error entries. **Two more real, pre-existing, separate issues found but deliberately not fixed here** (out of this item's own scope, flagged as new Pending items below): `List.groupBy` has the identical missing-`stdlib_param_names`-entry gap fix (1) above just fixed for these four functions, meaning a struct-keyed `List.groupBy` callback is equally exposed to the same class of bug — never exercised until now because nothing had previously combined an unannotated-lambda-needing-hint stdlib function outside `BOXED_ABI_CALLEES` with a struct key; and a hand-written comparator over struct list elements passed directly to `List.sort` was independently confirmed still broken (`member reference base type 'int64_t' is not a structure or union`) — `List.sort` isn't in `BOXED_ABI_CALLEES` and this item's own comparator synthesis sidesteps that bug by construction rather than fixing it. **21 new unit tests**, all real (parse/typecheck/lower/generate real `.cto` source, no mocking): 7 in `crates/typeck/src/tests.rs` (Int/Float keys accepted; Text key on a plain list is E0710 for all four functions; the struct+Float-field-key false-positive regression, registering `List.sortBy`/`minBy`/`maxBy`/`sumBy` directly against `certo_typeck`'s own `Ty`/`TypeEnv` API rather than pulling in the `certo-stdlib` crate as a dev-dependency, which would build a second, incompatible copy of `certo-typeck` itself — confirmed directly via a real `expected TypeEnv, found TypeEnv` error before backing out that approach), 6 in `crates/hir/src/tests.rs` (return-type recovery for all four functions via `generic_container_ret`; the struct+Text-field-key E0601 case; the struct+Float-field-key regression at the HIR level too), 5 in `crates/codegen/src/tests.rs` (real generated-C fragment checks: `__sort_by_cmp_`/`certo_list_sort` for `sortBy`, struct-element `.price` unboxing compiles, `minBy`/`maxBy` reuse the comparator then call `certo_list_first`/`certo_list_last`, `sumBy` synthesizes a named `__sum_by_` loop). Full `cargo test --workspace` clean (the one pre-existing `xeq` failure predates this item entirely, unrelated). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**, not just unit tests: `List.sortBy([5,3,1,4,2], (x) => x)` → `1 2 3 4 5`; `List.minBy`/`maxBy`/`sumBy` on the same list → `1`/`5`/`15`; a `List<Product>` (struct, `{name: Text, price: Float}`) keyed by `(p) => p.price` for all four functions → correct sort order, correct min/max element, correct sum; `List.sortBy(products, (p) => p.name)` (Text field key on a struct) correctly rejected with `E0601`; `certo check --explain` prints the new E0710 entry correctly. | `crates/stdlib`, `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir`, `crates/mir`, `crates/codegen`, `docs/STDLIB-QUICKREF.md`, `docs/ERROR-REFERENCE.md` |
| 170/171 | Investigated item 170 as filed (`List.groupBy` "has the identical `stdlib_param_names` gap item 162b fixed") — **that specific claim turned out to be wrong**, confirmed by direct repro before touching any code: `List.groupBy` is a `BOXED_ABI_CALLEES` member, so its callback lambda's param type comes from an entirely separate, independent mechanism (`crates/mir/src/lower.rs`'s `lower_lambda_boxed`/`elem_ty_hint`, resolved from the scrutinee list's element type at MIR time, not HIR's `stdlib_param_names`-gated hint) — a struct-keyed `List.groupBy(products, (p) => p.category)` already compiled and ran correctly with no fix, unlike `sortBy`/`minBy`/`maxBy`/`sumBy`, which weren't `BOXED_ABI_CALLEES` members and depended entirely on the mechanism item 162b fixed. Reported this finding back before proceeding, rather than either fixing a non-bug or silently dropping the item. Investigating it, however, surfaced a real, different, confirmed bug — item 171, done in the same slice per direction from that finding: **typeck's `Expr::App` arm only ever resolved a callee written as `Expr::Path`** (a bare identifier) when deriving `fn_name`/`fn_full_path` — a module-qualified call (`List.sortBy(...)`, `List.groupBy(...)`, any `Type.method(...)`) parses as `Expr::Field`, so `fn_name` was silently `None` for every such call. Confirmed the concrete, practical impact directly: `List.groupBy(key: (x) => x, list: xs)` — labeled arguments in non-declared order — type-checked as if the labels didn't exist, comparing the closure against the `list: List<T>` slot and the list against the `key: T=>K` slot, both wrongly. Fixed at the source (`crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs`'s `Expr::App` arm now matches `Expr::Field` too, reusing the existing `qualified_call_name` helper `resolve_callee` already used for the identical, narrower gap) rather than the item-162b-era workaround of re-deriving the name locally just for the E0710 check — that check's own re-derivation was simplified back to use the now-correctly-widened shared `fn_name`. Verified the widen was safe for the dominant case (positional calls, no labels, `args.len() >= params.len()`): the labeled-reordering branch's own logic is provably identical to plain positional inference there, so nothing regresses — confirmed by the full test suite staying green. **Investigating item 171's fix surfaced one more real, confirmed, but narrow bug**, again not attempted here — labeled `List.groupBy(...)` type-checked correctly after the fix above, but then *lowered* its arguments in written (unreordered) source order regardless, since typeck's own reordering (item 171) and HIR's separate, independent reordering (gated by `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`'s own `stdlib_param_names()` table — the mechanism item 170 originally, incorrectly, thought was the whole story) are two different tables serving two different compiler stages; `List.groupBy` genuinely was missing from *this* one, confirmed via a real compile failure (`passing 'certo_fn_t' to parameter of incompatible type 'CertoList *'`) — fixed with the one-line `stdlib_param_names()` addition item 170 originally proposed, just for the real reason surfaced by 171's own fix rather than the originally-guessed one. Writing the verification test for *that* fix surfaced a fourth, narrower, deliberately-not-fixed issue — split out as new item 172. 4 new unit tests, all real: 2 in `crates/typeck/src/tests.rs` (a `List.slice(to:, list:, from:)` call with labels out of declared order type-checks correctly; the same call with a wrong-typed labeled arg after reordering still correctly produces E0200 — not just "labels are silently ignored and it happens to pass"), 1 in `crates/codegen/src/tests.rs` (a labeled `List.groupBy` call's generated C passes the list operand before the closure operand to `certo_list_group_by`, matching the equivalent positional call — pinned at the call-site level specifically, not full-output equality, since a labeled call directly returned as a function's own tail expression hits item 172's separate quirk). Full `cargo test --workspace` clean. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `List.groupBy(key: (x) => x, list: [1,2,3])` (bound to a `val`, the realistic shape) correctly produces 3 groups; `List.slice(to: 3, list: xs, from: 1)` correctly reorders and slices. | `crates/typeck`, `crates/hir`, `crates/codegen` |

| 163 | Fixed both parts of the spec's §9.3 mismatch — `Text.join`'s argument order and the fictional regex-on-`Text` API — as documentation corrections, not code changes | Investigated first: confirmed real usages of `Text.join` exist nowhere in the repo (zero risk either way), and that the current `Text.join(list, sep)` order is the one internally consistent with the rest of this codebase's own collection-first convention (`List.map(list, f)`, `List.fold(list, init, f)`, `List.push(list, item)`, etc.) — the spec's `Text.join(sep, list)` was the inconsistent one, not the shipped code. Fixed the spec tables in both `docs/CERTO-SPEC.md` and `docs/Certo_Language_Specification.md` to `Text.join(list, sep)`, matching what `docs/GUIDE.md`/`docs/STDLIB-QUICKREF.md` already correctly documented — no code change, since the working, tested implementation was already right. For regex: confirmed (again) that the real API lives entirely under a separate `Regex.*` namespace (`Regex.match(pattern, text)`/`find`/`captures`/`replace`/`split`) with pattern-first argument order and plain `Text`/`Bool`/`List<Text>` returns — no `Match` type exists anywhere, and building real dot-call method syntax on `Text` (the other way to honor the spec's literal `text.matches(regex)` framing) depends entirely on item 162's own unresolved, much bigger dot-call-on-values design question, which is explicitly out of scope here. Replaced both spec files' `text.matches`/`text.find`/`text.replace`-as-regex rows with an accurate `Regex.*` table (signatures and descriptions ported directly from `docs/STDLIB-QUICKREF.md`'s own already-correct, already-shipped documentation), rather than leaving the spec describing an API that doesn't exist under any name. Verified no other references to the fictional `text.matches`/`.find(regex)` framing remain anywhere in either spec file. Purely a documentation fix — no unit tests apply; `Text.join`'s real behavior was already covered by existing stdlib tests, unchanged here. | `docs/CERTO-SPEC.md`, `docs/Certo_Language_Specification.md` |

| 148 | Rewrote spec §6 (Database Integration) to match the real, shipped DB API — a documentation-accuracy fix, not new compiler surface. Rewrote the section's code blocks as proper fenced ` ```certo `/` ```toml `/` ```powershell ` blocks rather than continuing the single-row-markdown-table pseudo-code-block convention the rest of this spec document uses (an artifact of a Word-doc paste-through that mis-renders `|>`-containing Certo source, since a bare `|` inside a one-cell table is a real column delimiter) — scoped to §6 only, not a pass over the rest of the document, since the goal was specifically to make the database section trustworthy as a copy-paste reference. No compiler/runtime code changed — this is a docs-only fix. | `docs/Certo_Language_Specification.md` |
| 149 | Spec §8.4/§8.5's three distinguishing domain-modeling constructs now all work: `@valueObject`/`@aggregate` marker annotations (item 149), in-body `fn` methods inside a `type` record body (item 150), and `expr.with(field: value, ...)` immutable copy-update syntax (item 151) | Implemented together since all three are the constructs the spec's own `ShoppingCart` aggregate example needs (`computed`, the example's fourth distinguishing construct, is item 143 — still Pending, not touched here). **`@valueObject`/`@aggregate`** (`crates/parser/src/parse_decl.rs`'s new `parse_type_marker_annotations`, `TypeDecl.annotations: Vec<String>`): recognized by exact name only — the same narrow hardcoded-shape precedent `@export`/`@ui.generate` already establish, not a general `@`-decorator system — and rejected with a real parse error if written before anything other than `type`. Accepted-and-ignored per the original investigation's own finding (neither is shown to change compiled behavior anywhere in the spec's examples): no typeck/hir/mir/codegen consumer reads them, but `crates/fmt/src/fmt_decl.rs` round-trips them (one per line, immediately before `type`, matching source). **In-body `fn` methods** (item 150) — the harder design question this item's own Pending note flagged (\"is the spec's in-body syntax genuine, or should the spec show `impl` instead?\") was resolved as: genuine syntax, desugared to the existing mechanism. `parse_record_type_def` now recognizes a `fn`/`pub fn`/`async fn` inside a record body and collects it separately from plain/computed fields; `parse_decl`'s own `Token::Type` arm then synthesizes a trailing `ImplDecl` from those methods (a real `impl TypeName { ... }` block, using the type's own name and type params) and returns both declarations — every later compiler stage (resolve/typeck/hir/mir/codegen) sees exactly the AST shape a hand-written `impl` block already produces, so **zero changes were needed anywhere downstream of the parser**. This is why `parse_decl` (and every one of its 20-odd match arms) now returns `Vec<S<Decl>>` instead of a single `S<Decl>` — confirmed this was the only structural ripple, and `parse_module.rs`'s own top-level loop needed exactly a one-line `decls.push(d)` → `decls.extend(ds)` change to match. This language has no `self`/implicit-receiver sugar anywhere (confirmed via every existing `impl` block, which always spells the receiver as an ordinary named-and-typed parameter) — an in-body method's receiver is no different, and is called the same way any other `impl` method already is (`Cart.addAmount(c, 10)`, not dot-call syntax, which item 162 already found doesn't exist for lowercase values). **`expr.with(field: value, ...)`** (item 151, `crates/parser/src/parse_expr.rs`'s postfix-position parsing): desugars directly to the *already-working* `Expr::Record` base-spread mechanism (the same one `TypeName { ..base, field: value }` already used) with `ty_name: None`, since the receiver's type name isn't spelled out at the `.with(...)` call site — typeck/HIR both already recover it from the base expression's own resolved type for ordinary record-spread, so this needed no new typeck/hir/mir/codegen work either, only the new parse rule. 11 new unit tests, all real (no mocking): 5 in `crates/parser/src/tests.rs` for the annotations (recorded on `TypeDecl`, empty when absent, rejected before a non-`type` decl, doesn't interfere with `@export`), 5 more there for in-body methods (desugars to a trailing `ImplDecl`; a record with none produces no extra decl; multiple methods group into *one* synthesized `impl`, not one per method; `pub fn` is recorded as `pub`; a generic record's type params carry through to the synthesized `impl`), 1 in `crates/fmt/src/tests.rs` (annotation round-trip), plus the pre-existing `dbschema`/`fmt` test fixtures updated for `TypeDecl`'s new field. `expr.with(...)` itself has no dedicated parser unit test — verified directly end-to-end instead. Full workspace test suite clean (`cargo test --workspace`). **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**, all three together: `@aggregate type Cart = { total: Int, fn addAmount(c: Cart, amount: Int): Cart = c.with(total: c.total + amount) }` parses, type-checks, and `Cart.addAmount(Cart { total: 5 }, 10)` correctly produces `15`. | `crates/ast`, `crates/parser`, `crates/fmt`, `crates/dbschema` |
| 172 | A module-qualified stdlib call's inline-lambda argument now gets its per-call-site type hint (`needs_lambda_hint`, `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`) even when the call uses labeled arguments — previously only the positional branch applied it, so `List.map`/`groupBy`/`sortBy`/`minBy`/`maxBy`/`sumBy`'s callback stayed `Ty::Error` whenever called with labels | Found while writing verification tests for items 170/171 (Completed table): `List.groupBy(key: (x) => x, list: [1, 2, 3])` directly returned as a function's own declared-type tail expression produced a wrong C return-type declaration, because `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`'s `Expr::App` lowering only ever called `lower_lambda_with_param_hint` inside the *positional* branch of `if has_labels { ... } else { needs_lambda_hint applies here }` — the labeled-reordering branch lowered every argument via plain `lower_expr`, with no hint at all, so the lambda's param/body stayed `Ty::Error` regardless of what its body could otherwise resolve to. **Fix** (`crates/hir/src/lower.rs`'s `Expr::App` handling, `has_labels` branch): restructured to compute each written argument's target slot index first (an exact mirror of the existing label/positional-cursor assignment algorithm, just deferring the actual `lower_expr` call so lowering order can differ from written order), then — for the six `needs_lambda_hint` functions only — lowers whichever argument landed in the `list` slot *first*, regardless of where it was written, and uses its resolved element type as the hint when lowering whichever argument landed in the `f`/`key` slot; every other slot lowers exactly as before (plain `lower_expr`). The positional (non-labeled) branch is untouched — this only closes the labeled-argument gap. The existing `is_key_fn`/E0601 struct-field-key validation (item 162b) is preserved unchanged in the labeled path, now actually able to run against a correctly-hinted lambda body instead of a permanently-`Ty::Error` one. 6 new unit tests, all real: 4 in `crates/hir/src/tests.rs` (`labeled_groupby_call_with_key_before_list_gets_lambda_hint`, `labeled_sortby_call_with_struct_float_key_before_list_lowers_ok`, `labeled_sortby_call_with_struct_text_key_before_list_is_e0601`, `labeled_map_call_with_f_before_list_gets_lambda_hint` — each writes the lambda-slot label before the list-slot label, the exact ordering that exposed the bug), 2 in `crates/codegen/src/tests.rs` (`groupby_labeled_call_as_direct_tail_expression_matches_positional_return_type` — the precise repro flagged, not fixed, in items 170/171's own writeup, now asserting the labeled call's generated `certo_run(...)` signature is byte-identical to the positional call's). Full `cargo test --workspace` clean, no regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: a function `groupIt(xs): Map<Int, List<Int>> = List.groupBy(key: (x) => x, list: xs)` (labels reversed from declared order, directly returned, no intermediate `val`) now compiles and runs correctly — grouping `[1,1,2,3,3,3]` by identity and reading back each group's length via `Map.get` produced `2` and `3`, the correct group sizes for keys `1` and `3`. | `crates/hir`, `crates/codegen` |
| 173 | A `computed` property's body (item 143) can now reference *other* `computed` properties on the same record, not just stored fields — `computed summary: Text = floatToText(total) ++ (if hasDiscount then "..." else "")` (both `total`/`hasDiscount` themselves computed) now resolves correctly instead of failing with `E0206: undefined name` | Item 143's synthesized accessor method prelude unconditionally binds one `val f = self.f` per *stored* field, but never bound other computed names — the fix deliberately avoids the naive "bind every other computed property too" approach flagged as out-of-scope when this item was filed, since that would mean evaluating every computed property on the record any time *any one* of them is read, regardless of whether the body being lowered actually references it. **Fix** (`crates/parser/src/parse_decl.rs`): added `collect_referenced_idents`, a recursive textwalk over the already-parsed computed body's `S<Expr>` tree that collects every bare (single-segment) identifier referenced anywhere in it — covering every `Expr`/`Stmt` variant (block statements, match arms/guards, lambdas, f-string interpolation, etc.), exhaustively checked by the compiler's own non-exhaustive-match error during development. `synthesize_computed_method` now takes the record's full sibling `computed` list and, after the unconditional stored-field bindings, adds one more `val other = self.other` for each *other* computed name (excluding the property's own name) that the referenced-identifier set actually contains — deliberately approximate (it doesn't distinguish a genuinely free reference from one shadowed by an inner `val`/lambda-param of the same name), since an extra unused prelude binding is harmless, the same tradeoff the pre-existing unconditional stored-field prelude already accepts. Because `other` is a real computed name, HIR's own `Expr::Field` computed-field fallback (`crates/hir/src/lower.rs`, item 143's own mechanism) already lowers that prelude read to a real call to `TypeName.other(self)` — so this needed zero new evaluation logic anywhere downstream of the parser. **Self-reference handling, a deliberate design choice**: a computed property's own name is always excluded from the candidate list, so `computed loopy: Int = loopy + 1` gets no binding for `loopy` and instead fails as an ordinary `E0206` undefined-name error — confirmed directly via `certo check` — rather than compiling into guaranteed infinite recursion (the prelude binding would otherwise re-call the very method being defined, unconditionally, on every invocation). A genuine *cycle* between two different computed properties (A referencing B, B referencing A) is **not** statically caught — flagged explicitly as an accepted limitation, matching this language's existing lack of general mutual-recursion-without-a-base-case detection; it would only actually recurse forever if such a cyclic accessor were ever called. 3 new unit tests in `crates/parser/src/tests.rs`: `computed_referencing_another_computed_gets_an_extra_prelude_binding` (a genuine cross-reference gets the extra binding), `computed_not_referencing_other_computed_fields_gets_no_extra_bindings` (an unrelated sibling computed property is correctly *not* bound — confirming this isn't the rejected "bind everything" approach), `computed_referencing_itself_does_not_get_a_self_binding` (no `val loopy = self.loopy` is synthesized). Full `cargo test --workspace` clean, no regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**, the exact scenario item 143's own writeup flagged as failing: `Order`'s `computed summary: Text = floatToText(total) ++ (if hasDiscount then " (discounted)" else "")`, referencing two sibling computed properties (`total`, `hasDiscount`), now both type-checks and runs correctly — `Order { subtotal: 100.0, taxRate: 0.1, discount: 5.0 }.summary` produced `"105 (discounted)"`, `Order { subtotal: 100.0, taxRate: 0.1, discount: 0.0 }.summary` produced `"110"`; a separate self-referencing `computed loopy: Int = loopy + 1` correctly failed at `certo check` time with `E0206` instead of compiling. | `crates/parser` |
| 174 | A record literal (`TypeName { field: value, ... }`) or `.with(field: value, ...)` copy-update (item 151) now validates that every field name it sets actually exists on the type — an unknown/typo'd field name is rejected immediately at `certo check` time with a real `E0205`, for both construction paths | Generalizes item 143's own narrow `E0217`-only check (computed-field rejection) to the *general* case, closing a real, alarming, pre-existing gap found while testing that check: before this fix, a typo'd field name in a direct literal only surfaced two compiler stages later as a confusing raw C error (`field designator does not refer to any field`), and the identical typo via `.with(...)` was **silently dropped with zero error anywhere** — confirmed directly via a real end-to-end run that printed success despite the assignment doing nothing. Fixed in `crates/typeck/src/infer_expr.rs`'s `Expr::Record` inference: replaced item 143's computed-only check with one pass that, for whichever nominal type is being constructed (`ty_name` for a direct literal, or `base_named` — the base's own resolved `Ty::Named`, recovered for `.with(...)`, which never has a syntactic `ty_name` per item 151's own design — exactly one of the two is ever present), walks every field in the literal/`.with(...)` and classifies each name against that type's declared fields: a name matching a real stored field is left alone (existing unification logic, unchanged); a name matching a `computed` property raises the existing `E0217` (`ComputedFieldNotSettable`); any other name raises the pre-existing `E0205`/`TypeErrorKind::UnknownField` — reused rather than inventing a new error code, since the semantics ("no such field") are identical to the existing read-side E0205 check, just triggered from the construction site instead of a field-read site. Deliberately skips the check entirely when the type isn't a known record (`record_fields.get(tn)` returns `None` — e.g. a statemachine type), matching `resolve_field_ty`'s own conservative fallback pattern. `docs/ERROR-REFERENCE.md`'s existing E0205 entry updated with two new examples (literal and `.with(...)`) and a note pointing to E0217 for the computed-field case specifically. 4 new unit tests in `crates/typeck/src/tests.rs` (`unknown_field_in_a_literal_is_e0205`, `unknown_field_via_with_is_e0205`, `partial_with_update_of_real_fields_is_still_ok`, `full_literal_construction_with_only_real_fields_is_still_ok`) — `cargo test -p certo-typeck` went from 156 to 165 passing, 0 failed. Full `cargo test --workspace` clean — **no regressions**, confirming no existing test relied on the old permissive/silent behavior, and every item 143 computed-field test (E0217 still fires, not downgraded to E0205) and every legitimate construction/`.with()` case (real fields, partial `.with()` updates, generic records) still passes unchanged. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: `Order { total: 5, totall: 10 }` (direct-literal typo) now correctly fails `certo check` with `E0205` instead of only failing at the C-compiler stage; `o.with(totall: 10)` (`.with()` typo) now correctly fails with `E0205` instead of silently compiling and running with no effect; a genuine computed-field-name construction attempt still correctly produces `E0217`, not `E0205`. | `crates/typeck`, `docs/ERROR-REFERENCE.md` |
| 175 | `certo fmt` now round-trips a `type` record body containing in-body `fn` methods (item 150) and/or `computed` properties (item 143) correctly — no more duplicated output from the parser's synthesized trailing `impl` block | Investigated first, since the item's own two originally-proposed fixes (a second non-desugaring parser entry point, or heuristic "clearly synthesized" impl detection) turned out to both be more than necessary: the parser already retains a `computed` property's original, un-desugared body in `RecordTypeDef.computed` (item 143's own design), and `fmt_type_decl`'s Record branch was already printing it correctly — the actual bug was that `fmt_module` *also* printed the parser's synthesized trailing `ImplDecl` as a second, separate top-level declaration, duplicating every computed accessor (which would fail to recompile — two definitions of the same method) and, for in-body `fn` methods (which have no redundant copy anywhere else), visually moving them out of the `type { ... }` body into a separate `impl` block. **Fix**: rather than heuristically detecting "this impl looks synthesized," the parser now says so explicitly. Two new AST fields (`crates/ast/src/decl.rs`): `ImplDecl.is_synthesized: bool` (`true` only for the trailing impl `parse_decl`'s `Token::Type` arm builds from a type's in-body methods/computed accessors; `false` for every hand-written `impl X { ... }` block, parsed via `parse_impl_decl`), and `RecordTypeDef.methods: Vec<FnDecl>` (retains the in-body `fn` methods in their original, un-desugared form — mirroring how `computed` already retains its own originals — read only by `fmt`; every other compiler stage continues to consume the equivalent copies on the synthesized `ImplDecl`, unchanged). `crates/parser/src/parse_decl.rs`: `parse_record_type_def` now also stores the parsed in-body methods onto `RecordTypeDef.methods`; the two `ImplDecl` construction sites set `is_synthesized: true`/`false` respectively. `crates/fmt`: `fmt_module.rs` skips any `Decl::Impl` with `is_synthesized: true` entirely (its content is already represented by the origin `type`'s own body); `fmt_decl.rs`'s `fmt_type_decl` Record branch now also prints `rec.methods` (in-body `fn`s) inside the `type { ... }` block, alongside `fields`/`computed` — matching the shape the code was actually written in, not a separate top-level `impl`. Only ~3 real call sites needed updating for the two new struct fields (`crates/parser/src/parse_decl.rs` ×2, `crates/dbschema/src/tests.rs` ×1 test helper), confirmed exhaustively via compiler errors, the same mechanical, low-risk shape as item 143's own `RecordTypeDef.computed` addition. 4 new unit tests in `crates/fmt/src/tests.rs`: `in_body_fn_method_round_trips_inside_type_body_no_duplicate_impl`, `computed_property_round_trips_without_duplicate_impl_block`, `type_with_both_in_body_fn_and_computed_round_trips_without_duplicate_impl` (all assert no `impl TypeName {` appears in the output), and a regression test `hand_written_standalone_impl_block_still_prints_normally` (confirming only *synthesized* impls are skipped, not real ones). Full `cargo test --workspace` clean, no regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**: formatting a file with `type Invoice = { paidAt: Text?; total: Int; computed isPaid: Bool = Option.isSome(paidAt); fn addTotal(inv: Invoice, amount: Int): Int = inv.total + amount }` now produces a diff touching *only* unrelated formatting elsewhere in the file — the `type` block itself is byte-identical to the original source, with no separate `impl Invoice { ... }` appended; the formatted output correctly re-parses, re-typechecks (`certo check` → `ok`), and rebuilds into a working binary whose output (`true 15` for `isPaid`/`addTotal(inv, 5)` on a `total: 10` invoice) matches the pre-format source exactly. **New, more severe, entirely unrelated bug found while verifying this end-to-end, not fixed here** — flagged as new item 176 below: `crates/fmt/src/fmt_expr.rs`'s `Expr::Record` formatting arm destructures `{ base, fields, .. }`, silently discarding `ty_name` — so `certo fmt` drops the type name from **every** reformatted record literal, not just ones involved in items 143/150/175, confirmed via the minimal repro `Point { x: 1, y: 2 }` (no computed/in-body methods at all) reformatting to the bare, unparseable `{ x: 1, y: 2 }`. This is a pre-existing defect in `fmt_expr.rs`, which item 175's own fix never touches (that file has no changes in this item's diff) — confirmed by reproducing it against the unmodified formatter. | `crates/ast`, `crates/parser`, `crates/fmt`, `crates/dbschema` |
| 176 | `certo fmt` no longer drops the type name from a reformatted record literal, nor mangles a `.with(...)` copy-update into invalid syntax — both previously produced output that failed to re-parse | Investigating confirmed the item's own original writeup had under-scoped the fix: it assumed the `.with(...)`/base-only case was "already correct" (`base with { ... }`), but a direct repro showed that's *also* invalid syntax — this language has no bare `expr with { ... }` expression grammar at all (confirmed via grep: `Token::With` is only ever consumed directly after `Token::Dot`, i.e. `.with(`), so reformatting `o.with(status: "closed")` produced `o with { status: "closed" }`, which fails to parse identically to the type-name-dropping bug (`expected declaration, found With`). Both are the same root cause — `crates/fmt/src/fmt_expr.rs`'s `Expr::Record` arm destructured `{ base, fields, .. }`, discarding `ty_name` entirely and using an invented `with` keyword that was never real syntax — so fixed together in one pass. **Fix**: the arm now matches on `(ty_name, base)` explicitly, covering all four real shapes this one AST node represents: `(Some(name), None)` → `TypeName { field: value }` (direct literal); `(Some(name), Some(b))` → `TypeName { ..base, field: value }` (the real spread grammar, `parse_expr.rs`'s `parse_record_body`, distinct from `.with()` — confirmed `ty_name`/`base` really can co-occur for this form); `(None, Some(b))` → `base.with(field: value)` (item 151's postfix desugar, which never has a syntactic `ty_name`); `(None, None)` → no real source form exists for a bare, unnamed record literal in this language (item 145) — formatted permissively rather than panicking. 3 new unit tests in `crates/fmt/src/tests.rs`: `record_literal_keeps_its_type_name`, `with_copy_update_round_trips_as_dot_with_call`, `record_spread_with_explicit_type_name_round_trips`. Full `cargo test --workspace` clean, no regressions. **Verified end-to-end via the real compiled `certo.exe`**, both original repros: `type Point = { x: Int, y: Int }` / `val p = Point { x: 1, y: 2 }` now formats with `Point` preserved and the output correctly re-parses (`certo check` → `ok`); `type Order = { total: Int, status: Text }` / `o.with(status: "closed")` now formats as `o.with(status: "closed")` (unchanged), correctly re-parses, rebuilds, and runs — printing `5` for `o2.total` (the field `.with()` didn't touch, confirming the copy-update itself still behaves correctly after the round-trip, not just that it parses). | `crates/fmt` |
| 145 | Anonymous (no leading type name) record patterns now parse and work correctly in both `val` destructuring and `match` — spec §3.1 (`val { name, email } = user`) and §3.4 (`match user { { name, status: Active } => ... }`) | Investigation found the item's own filed scope was wrong in a significant way: the "real typeck design question" it worried about (which type does a bare `{}` pattern bind against?) was **already answered and already implemented** — `Pattern::Record`'s AST already has `path: Option<ModulePath>`, and typeck's `check_pattern` already falls back to deriving the type name from the already-known `expected` type when `path` is `None`; same for `resolve`'s pattern-binding walk. Neither needed any change. The real blockers, found while verifying the type-prefixed form the item's own note claimed "already works correctly," were two separate, more severe, pre-existing bugs: (1) **`match` on *any* record pattern was never implemented** — `crates/hir/src/lower.rs`'s `lower_pat` explicitly flattened `Pattern::Record` to `HirPat::Wildcard` with a `// full pattern compilation later` comment; confirmed via direct repro that this doesn't fail cleanly, it silently drops every field binding and only fails two stages later at the **raw C compiler** with `use of undeclared identifier 'certo_n'`.
